CHAPTER 22
Database & Persistence Layer
The 28-table schema that stores off-chain state, complementing the on-chain record with
queryable, indexed data.
The blockchain stores immutable transaction records, but it is not designed for the
complex queries that the application layer needs — list all assets in a category, find
all transactions for a user in a date range, compute aggregate economy statistics.
Averon uses sql.js (SQLite compiled to WebAssembly) for off-chain state, with 28
tables that mirror and extend the on-chain data.
8.1 Technology Choice: sql.js
Averon uses sql.js — SQLite compiled to WebAssembly — as its database engine. This is an unusual
choice for a production blockchain platform; most use PostgreSQL or MongoDB. The choice is
deliberate and based on three considerations.
Zero native dependencies. sql.js is a pure WebAssembly module with no C compilation
requirements, no shared library dependencies, and no system-level installation. This eliminates an entire
category of deployment issues — there is no need to install PostgreSQL, configure authentication,
manage connection pools, or troubleshoot version mismatches. The database runs in the same process as
the application, communicating via JavaScript API calls rather than network round-trips.
In-process execution. Because sql.js runs in the same process as the application, there are no
network round-trips to a database server. This dramatically reduces latency for database operations —
what would be a 1-5 millisecond network round-trip to PostgreSQL becomes a sub-microsecond
in-process call. For Averon's write patterns (sequential state transitions, not concurrent high-throughput
writes), this latency advantage is significant.
Easy deployment. The entire database is a single .db file that can be backed up by copying, moved
between servers by transferring, and inspected with standard SQLite tools. There is no separate database
server to manage, monitor, or scale. This dramatically simplifies operations, particularly for smaller
deployments that do not have a dedicated database administrator.

---END---
PAGE_146
AVERON
Rishabh Gupta
AVERON PROTOCOL SERIES  ·  VOL. I
145
The trade-off is concurrency. SQLite has a single writer lock, meaning that only one write operation
can execute at a time. This would be a fatal limitation for a high-throughput application like a social
network, but Averon's write patterns are sequential state transitions (asset moves from draft to verified
to active to funded), not concurrent high-throughput writes. The single-writer lock is rarely a
bottleneck, and when it is, the application can be sharded across multiple Averon instances (each with
its own sql.js database) synchronized via the blockchain.
8.2 Persistence Strategy
sql.js operates entirely in memory — it does not write to disk automatically. Averon implements a
periodic persistence strategy: every three seconds, the in-memory database is serialized and written to
disk via atomic file replacement. Atomic file replacement uses the temp-file-and-rename pattern: the
database is written to a temporary file, then the temporary file is renamed to the final filename.
Renaming is atomic on POSIX filesystems, which means that the database file on disk is always either
the previous version or the new version — never a partially-written corrupt state.
On startup, the database is loaded from disk into memory. If the database file does not exist (fresh
deployment), all 28 tables are created with CREATE TABLE IF NOT EXISTS statements. The
economy table is seeded with default values (initial price, zero circulating supply, zero total raised), and
the system_config table is seeded with default configuration values. The auto-persist timer is then
started, ensuring that subsequent writes are persisted to disk.
All writes happen inside BEGIN / COMMIT / ROLLBACK transactions, ensuring atomic state
transitions. If any statement in a transaction fails, the entire transaction is rolled back, leaving the
database in its pre-transaction state. This is essential for Averon's asset lifecycle state machine — when
an asset moves from one state to another, the state change, the audit log entry, and any related
blockchain transaction must all succeed together or all fail together.
8.3 Schema Overview
The Averon database has 28 tables, organized into six domains. The domain organization reflects the
platform's architecture — each domain corresponds to a layer or service in the system. Tables within a
domain are tightly coupled; tables across domains communicate via foreign keys but are otherwise
independent.

---END---
PAGE_147
AVERON
Rishabh Gupta
AVERON PROTOCOL SERIES  ·  VOL. I
146
Figure 8.1 — Database Schema: 28 Tables Grouped by Domain. The six domains are User & Auth, Asset & Tokenization,
Trading & Economy, Payments & KYC, System & Audit, and Infrastructure. Each table is created with CREATE TABLE
IF NOT EXISTS at startup.
8.4 User and Auth Domain
The User and Auth domain has five tables: users, sessions, wallets, notifications, and activity_log. These
tables store user accounts, authentication state, wallet key material, user-facing notifications, and a feed
of user activity for the dashboard.
The users table is the central identity table. Each row has: id (primary key, auto-incremented),
email (unique, validated format), password_hash (PBKDF2-SHA512, 100k iterations, 32-byte random
salt prepended), role (one of 'user', 'admin'), wallet_address (foreign key to wallets table), is_frozen
(boolean, defaults false), kyc_status (one of 'unverified', 'pending', 'verified', 'rejected'), created_at,
updated_at. The first user to register on a fresh deployment is automatically assigned the 'admin' role;
subsequent users are assigned the 'user' role.
The sessions table stores JWT refresh tokens. Each row has: id, user_id (foreign key),
refresh_token (the JWT string), expires_at (7 days from issuance), is_revoked (boolean, set to true on
logout or password change), created_at. Access tokens (15-minute expiry) are not stored in the database

---END---
PAGE_148
AVERON
Rishabh Gupta
AVERON PROTOCOL SERIES  ·  VOL. I
147
— they are stateless and verified via HMAC-SHA256 signature alone. Refresh tokens are stored to
support revocation, which is essential for security (a compromised refresh token can be revoked by the
user changing their password).
The wallets table stores ECDSA key pairs. Each row has: id, user_id (foreign key), public_key
(PEM SPKI format), private_key (PEM PKCS8 format), address (SHA-256 → RIPEMD-160 → hex →
0x prefix), created_at. One row per user, created at registration. The private key is stored in plaintext
(see the security trade-off discussion in Chapter 7).
The notifications table stores user-facing notifications. Each row has: id, user_id, type (one of
fourteen 
event 
types: 
BLOCK_MINED, 
TRADE_EXECUTED, 
ORDER_PLACED,
ORDER_CANCELLED, PRICE_UPDATED, ASSET_CREATED, ASSET_STATUS_CHANGED,
ASSET_FUNDED, 
TOKEN_PURCHASED, 
COINS_MINTED, 
USER_REGISTERED,
NOTIFICATION, ESCROW_LOCKED, ESCROW_RELEASED, CIRCUIT_BREAKER), title,
message, is_read (boolean), created_at. Notifications are pushed to clients via WebSocket and persisted
for later retrieval.
The activity_log table is a feed of user activity for the dashboard. Each row has: id, user_id, action
(string describing the action), details (JSON string with action-specific data), tx_hash (if the action
resulted in a blockchain transaction), amount (if the action involved a value transfer), created_at. The
activity log is queryable by user_id and date range, and is displayed in the user dashboard.
8.5 Asset and Tokenization Domain
The Asset and Tokenization domain has seven tables: assets, asset_documents, asset_status_history,
asset_valuations, asset_tokens, escrow_accounts, and escrow_transactions. These tables store the
complete asset lifecycle, from draft through completed or closed.
The assets table is the central asset table. Each row has: id, owner_id (foreign key to users), title,
description, category (one of twelve predefined categories), status (one of fourteen lifecycle states —
see Chapter 11), ai_verified (boolean), ai_valuation (decimal), ai_risk_score (integer 0-100),
ai_risk_level (one of 'low', 'medium', 'high', 'critical'), ai_confidence (decimal 0-100), ai_analysis
(text), ai_concerns (text), token_count (integer 2-10000), token_price (decimal, in both INR and AC),
escrow_address (foreign key to escrow_accounts), compliance_status (one of 'pending', 'approved',
'rejected'), raise_amount (decimal), listing_duration (integer, days), created_at, updated_at.
The asset_documents table stores uploaded verification documents. Each row has: id, asset_id
(foreign key), filepath (relative path within uploads/ directory), mimetype (one of JPG, PNG, WebP,

---END---
PAGE_149
AVERON
Rishabh Gupta
AVERON PROTOCOL SERIES  ·  VOL. I
148
PDF), doc_hash (SHA-256 hash for duplicate detection), uploaded_at. Maximum 10 documents per
asset, maximum 10 MB per document.
The asset_status_history table records every state transition. Each row has: id, asset_id, old_status,
new_status, changed_by (user_id of admin or system), reason (text describing why the transition
occurred), changed_at. This table is the audit trail for the asset lifecycle state machine.
The asset_valuations table stores AI valuation records. Each row has: id, asset_id, valuation
(decimal), risk_score (integer 0-100), confidence (decimal 0-100), source (one of 'gemini', 'fallback'),
created_at. Multiple valuations can exist per asset — the most recent is the current valuation.
The asset_tokens table stores fractional ownership tokens. Each row has: id, asset_id, token_index
(integer 1 to token_count), price (decimal), owner_id (foreign key to users, NULL if unclaimed),
tx_hash (blockchain transaction hash of the INVEST transaction that claimed this token), claimed_at.
The UPDATE WHERE owner_id IS NULL pattern (described in Chapter 13) is used to atomically
claim unclaimed tokens.
The escrow_accounts table stores per-asset escrow accounts. Each row has: id, asset_id, address
(the ESCROW_<assetId>_<timestamp> wallet address), balance (current AC balance), total_received,
total_released, total_refunded, created_at, closed_at. One row per asset, created when the asset moves
to compliance_review.
The escrow_transactions table records every escrow movement. Each row has: id,
escrow_account_id, type (one of 'LOCK', 'RELEASE', 'REFUND'), user_id, amount, tx_hash,
created_at. LOCK entries are created when investors purchase tokens; RELEASE entries are created
when the asset is fully funded and the payout is processed; REFUND entries are created when the asset
expires and investors are refunded.
8.6 Trading and Economy Domain
The Trading and Economy domain has five tables: coin_orders, coin_trades, economy, price_history,
and fee_ledger. These tables store the order book, executed trades, aggregate economy statistics,
historical price data, and the fee ledger.
The coin_orders table stores open and historical orders on the AVR trading exchange. Each row
has: id, user_id, side (one of 'buy', 'sell'), type (one of 'market', 'limit'), amount (decimal), price
(decimal, NULL for market orders), filled (decimal, amount already matched), remaining (decimal,
amount still on the book), status (one of 'open', 'partial', 'filled', 'cancelled'), created_at, updated_at.

---END---
PAGE_150
AVERON
Rishabh Gupta
AVERON PROTOCOL SERIES  ·  VOL. I
149
The coin_trades table stores executed trades. Each row has: id, buy_order_id, sell_order_id,
amount, price, buyer_fee, seller_fee, tx_hash (blockchain transaction hash of the TRADE transaction),
created_at. Each trade links to the buy and sell orders that were matched.
The economy table is a singleton row that stores global economy metrics. The row has: id (always
1), price (current AC price in INR), total_supply (total AC ever minted), circulating_supply (total AC
minus burned), total_raised_inr (sum of all asset raise amounts), market_cap (price times
circulating_supply), tvl (total value locked in escrow accounts), total_fees_collected, updated_at. This
row is updated on every state-changing operation.
The price_history table stores time-series price data for charting. Each row has: id, price, volume
(sum of trade amounts in the period), high, low, open, close, recorded_at. A new row is inserted every
time the price changes (via trade, mint, or natural fluctuation).
The fee_ledger table records every fee collected by the platform. Each row has: id, user_id,
fee_type (one of 'trading', 'capital_raise', 'withdrawal', 'gateway'), amount, reference_id (the order,
asset, or withdrawal that generated the fee), created_at. The sum of all amounts in this table equals
economy.total_fees_collected.
8.7 Payments and KYC Domain
The Payments and KYC domain has nine tables: payment_gateways, payment_orders,
payment_transactions, 
kyc_records, 
kyc_tier_history, 
daily_limits, 
withdrawal_requests,
settlement_batches, and reconciliation_log. These tables store payment gateway configurations,
individual payment orders, payment transaction audit trails, KYC document submissions, KYC tier
changes, daily transaction limits, withdrawal requests, settlement batches, and reconciliation records.
These tables are documented in detail in Chapter 16 (Payments) and Chapter 17 (KYC). The key
tables for understanding the platform are payment_orders (which tracks every fiat-to-AC purchase,
with status transitions: created → pending → verifying → completed / failed) and withdrawal_requests
(which tracks every AC-to-fiat withdrawal, with status transitions: pending → processing → completed /
failed).
8.8 System and Audit Domain
The System and Audit domain has four tables: audit_log, system_config, temp_config, and tem_confi
(a typo in the original schema that has been preserved for backward compatibility). These tables store
the tamper-proof audit log, persistent system configuration, and temporary configuration overrides.

---END---
PAGE_151
AVERON
Rishabh Gupta
AVERON PROTOCOL SERIES  ·  VOL. I
150
The audit_log table is the most security-critical table in the database. Each row has: id, action
(string describing the action), details (JSON string), prev_hash (SHA-256 hash of the previous audit log
entry), entry_hash (SHA-256 hash of this entry, computed from prev_hash + user_id + action +
resource_type + resource_id + timestamp), user_id, resource_type, resource_id, timestamp. The hash
chain makes the audit log tamper-evident — any modification to an entry breaks the chain and is
detectable via verifyAuditChain(). The audit log is documented in detail in Chapter 19.
The system_config table stores persistent configuration that can be changed by admins via the
/api/admin/config endpoint. Each row has: id, key (string), value (string), updated_by (user_id),
updated_at. Configuration keys include: trading_fee_percent (default 0.1), capital_raise_fee_percent
(default 1.0), withdrawal_fee_ac (default 0.5), circuit_breaker_threshold_percent (default 10),
circuit_breaker_window_minutes 
(default 
60), 
min_raise_amount_inr 
(default 
100),
max_raise_amount_inr (default 1000000000), and others.
8.9 Summary
This chapter has documented Averon's database layer: the technology choice (sql.js — SQLite WASM,
for zero native dependencies, in-process execution, and easy deployment), the persistence strategy
(auto-save every 3 seconds, atomic file replacement, BEGIN/COMMIT/ROLLBACK transactions), and
the 28-table schema organized into six domains (User & Auth, Asset & Tokenization, Trading &
Economy, Payments & KYC, System & Audit, Infrastructure). The full table inventory with columns
and purposes is in Appendix A. Chapter 9 moves to the service layer, which orchestrates business logic
across these tables.
KEY TAKEAWAYS
• sql.js (SQLite WASM) chosen for zero native dependencies, in-process execution, easy
deployment.
• Auto-persist every 3 seconds via atomic file replacement (temp file + rename).
• 28 tables organized into 6 domains: User & Auth, Asset & Tokenization, Trading &
Economy, Payments & KYC, System & Audit, Infrastructure.
• All writes happen inside BEGIN/COMMIT/ROLLBACK transactions for atomic state
transitions.
• audit_log table is hash-chained (prev_hash → entry_hash), making the audit trail
tamper-evident.

---END---

Rishabh Gupta

would it belong to, and what new tables would you create?

risk?
The audit_log table is hash-chained. What additional integrity guarantees would timestamping provide, and

CHAPTER 23
Service Layer Architecture
The 13 domain services that coordinate all business logic, communicating exclusively via the
in-memory event bus.
The service layer is where Averon's business logic lives. Each of the thirteen services
owns one domain — assets, trading, payments, KYC, escrow, settlement, pricing,
fees, compliance, documents, AI, event bus, and WebSocket — and exposes methods
that the API layer calls. Services do not call each other directly; they communicate
via the event bus.
9.1 The Thirteen Services
Averon v4.0.0 has thirteen services, each implemented as a singleton in backend/services/. The services
are initialized at startup (step 8 of the initialization sequence) and live for the process lifetime. Each
service has a clearly defined responsibility and a well-documented interface, making the system
modular and testable.

---END---
PAGE_154
AVERON
Rishabh Gupta
AVERON PROTOCOL SERIES  ·  VOL. I
153
Figure 9.1 — Service Layer: 13 Domain Services. Each service owns one business domain and publishes events to the
in-memory bus. Services communicate exclusively via the event bus — no direct calls.
The thirteen services are: aiPipeline (Gemini 2.0 multimodal analysis with fallback engine),
assetService (manages the fourteen-state asset lifecycle), tradingEngine (order book matching with
circuit breaker), paymentService (Razorpay and Stripe integration with double-mint prevention),
escrowService (per-asset escrow account management), kycService (four-tier KYC verification and
document management), settlementService (batch processing of withdrawals and payouts),
priceService (four-mechanism price engine), feeService (fee calculation and ledger management),
complianceService (KYC limits and AML flag screening), documentProcessor (file upload and
SHA-256 hash deduplication), eventBus (in-memory pub/sub singleton with fourteen event types), and
wsServer (WebSocket server with six channels and JWT-based authentication).
9.2 The Event Bus
The event bus is the connective tissue of the service layer. It is an in-memory pub/sub singleton
implemented 
in 
backend/services/eventBus.js. 
The 
bus 
supports 
fourteen 
event 
types:
BLOCK_MINED, 
TRADE_EXECUTED, 
ORDER_PLACED, 
ORDER_CANCELLED,
PRICE_UPDATED, 
ASSET_CREATED, 
ASSET_STATUS_CHANGED, 
ASSET_FUNDED,
TOKEN_PURCHASED, 
COINS_MINTED, 
USER_REGISTERED, 
NOTIFICATION,
ESCROW_LOCKED, ESCROW_RELEASED, and CIRCUIT_BREAKER.
The bus exposes two methods: emit(eventType, payload) publishes an event to all subscribers, and
subscribe(eventType, callback) registers a callback to be invoked when an event of the given type is
emitted. Subscriptions are in-memory only — they do not survive process restarts. This is acceptable
because the events represent real-time notifications, not persistent state; the underlying state is in the
database and on the blockchain, both of which are persistent.
The decision to use an in-memory event bus rather than a message queue (RabbitMQ, Kafka, etc.) is
deliberate. A message queue would add operational complexity (another service to deploy, monitor, and
scale) without providing meaningful benefit for Averon's use case. Averon is designed to run as a single
process; the event bus's role is to coordinate communication between services within that process, not to
coordinate communication between processes. For multi-process deployments, the blockchain itself
serves as the coordination mechanism — transactions mined into blocks are visible to all processes.
9.3 The Asset Service

---END---
PAGE_155
AVERON
Rishabh Gupta
AVERON PROTOCOL SERIES  ·  VOL. I
154
The asset service is the most complex service in Averon. It owns the fourteen-state asset lifecycle state
machine (documented in detail in Chapter 11) and coordinates with the AI pipeline, escrow service,
compliance service, fee service, and price service to move assets through their lifecycle. The service is
implemented in backend/services/assetService.js.
The asset service exposes methods for each lifecycle transition: createAsset (creates a draft asset),
uploadDocuments (transitions to documents_uploaded), analyzeAsset (triggers the AI pipeline,
transitions to ai_analyzing), confirmAsset (transitions to compliance_review then active if compliance
passes), buyTokens (transitions to funding on first purchase, funded on full funding),
processFullyFunded (transitions to payout_pending, processes payout, transitions to completed),
checkExpiry (transitions expired assets to refunding), processRefund (transitions to closed). Each
method is atomic — it either succeeds completely or fails completely, with no partial state changes.
9.4 The AI Pipeline Service
The AI pipeline service implements the five-stage verification pipeline documented in detail in Chapter
12. The service is implemented in backend/services/aiPipeline.js and exposes a single method:
analyzeAsset(assetId). This method runs the five stages — document ingestion, duplicate check, AI
analysis, fraud detection, tokenization recommendation — and returns a structured JSON payload with
verified, estimated_value, risk_score, risk_level, analysis, concerns, and confidence fields.
The primary AI engine is Google Gemini 2.0 Flash, called via the gemini-2.0-flash:generateContent
endpoint with a multi-modal request containing asset metadata and document images. The model is
prompted to return a JSON response with the fields above. If Gemini is unavailable (network error, rate
limit, API key issue), the service falls back to a category-specific engine that uses hardcoded valuation
profiles (e.g., Land: average value less than 5 lakh, risk 10-35%) with randomized adjustments for
document quality and raise-to-value ratio. The fallback ensures that asset verification can proceed even
when the AI service is down.
9.5 The Trading Engine Service
The trading engine service implements the order book matching algorithm documented in detail in
Chapter 15. The service is implemented in backend/services/tradingEngine.js and exposes methods:
placeOrder (creates a new buy or sell order, attempts to match it against the existing book), cancelOrder
(removes an open order from the book), getOrderBook (returns the current bid and ask sides of the
book).

---END---
PAGE_156
AVERON
Rishabh Gupta
AVERON PROTOCOL SERIES  ·  VOL. I
155
The matching algorithm uses price-time priority: the best price is matched first, and within the same
price, the earliest order is matched first. The maker's price wins — the sell order's price is the trade
price. Self-trading is prevented (buy.user_id equals sell.user_id is skipped). Partial fills are supported:
an order can be filled across multiple matches, with the remaining quantity staying on the book. Market
orders use the best available price or the current economy price.
The trading engine includes a circuit breaker that halts all trading if the price moves more than ten
percent within any one-hour window. The circuit breaker is configurable via system_config
(circuit_breaker_threshold_percent and circuit_breaker_window_minutes). When the circuit breaker
triggers, a CIRCUIT_BREAKER event is emitted on the event bus, the WebSocket server broadcasts a
halt notification to all subscribers on the 'trades' channel, and all new order submissions are rejected
until the window resets.
9.6 The Payment Service
The payment service implements the fiat-to-AC minting flow documented in detail in Chapter 16. The
service is implemented in backend/services/paymentService.js and exposes methods: createOrder
(creates a payment order with status 'created'), verifyPayment (verifies the fiat payment via the
gateway's API, transitions to 'verifying', mints AC if verification succeeds), handleWebhook (handles
webhook callbacks from the payment gateway), refundPayment (refunds a completed payment via the
gateway's refund API).
The payment service supports four gateways: Razorpay (INR, HMAC-SHA256 signature
verification), Stripe (multi-currency, PaymentIntent status verification), UPI (INR, admin
confirmation), and wire transfer (multi-currency, admin confirmation). The double-mint prevention
mechanism is critical: before minting AC, the service executes UPDATE payment_orders SET status =
'verifying' WHERE id = ? AND status IN ('created', 'pending'). If the affected row count is zero
(meaning the order was already being processed by a concurrent request), the service throws an error
and no coins are minted. This prevents the same fiat payment from being used to mint AC twice.
9.7 The Escrow Service
The escrow service manages per-asset escrow accounts. The service is implemented in
backend/services/escrowService.js and exposes methods: createEscrowAccount (creates a new
ESCROW_<assetId>_<timestamp> wallet for an asset), lockFunds (moves AVR from an investor's
wallet to the escrow wallet, records a LOCK entry in escrow_transactions), releaseFunds (moves 99%
of escrow balance to the asset owner, 1% to __PLATFORM_FEE__, records RELEASE entries),

---END---
PAGE_157
AVERON
Rishabh Gupta
AVERON PROTOCOL SERIES  ·  VOL. I
156
refundAll (moves the entire escrow balance back to investors proportionally, records REFUND entries),
getEscrowBalance (returns the current AC balance of an escrow account).
The escrow service is the trust mechanism of the asset lifecycle. Investor funds are held in escrow
— not by the asset owner — until the raise succeeds. If the raise fails (expiry before full funding), the
escrow service refunds all investors atomically. This design eliminates the counterparty risk that would
otherwise exist between investors and asset owners.
9.8 The KYC Service
The KYC service implements the four-tier verification system documented in detail in Chapter 17. The
service is implemented in backend/services/kycService.js and exposes methods: submitKYC (creates a
kyc_records entry with status 'pending'), verifyKYC (admin-only, approves or rejects a KYC
submission with reason, updates the user's kyc_tier if approved), getKYCStatus (returns the user's
current tier and submission history), checkTierLimits (checks whether a proposed transaction would
exceed the user's daily/monthly/annual limits for their tier).
The four tiers range from Tier 0 (Unverified, 20-rupee daily limit) to Tier 3 (Institutional, 10-crore
daily limit). Each tier has specific requirements: one document and three trades for Tier 1, ten trades
and one lakh volume for Tier 2, fifty trades and one crore volume for Tier 3. Tier changes are recorded
in kyc_tier_history with old_tier, new_tier, and reason. Daily transaction totals are tracked in
daily_limits to enforce per-day caps.
9.9 The Settlement Service
The settlement service handles batch processing of withdrawals and payouts. The service is
implemented in backend/services/settlementService.js and exposes methods: processWithdrawal
(admin-only, processes a single withdrawal request — signs TRANSFER and FEE transactions, mines
them into a block, updates database balances, initiates bank transfer), processSettlementBatch
(admin-only, processes a batch of withdrawal requests together for operational efficiency),
reconcileGateway (periodically checks for discrepancies between internal records and gateway records,
logs discrepancies to reconciliation_log).
9.10 The Price Service
The price service implements the four-mechanism price engine documented in detail in Chapter 14.
The service is implemented in backend/services/priceService.js and exposes methods: getCurrentPrice

---END---
PAGE_158
AVERON
Rishabh Gupta
AVERON PROTOCOL SERIES  ·  VOL. I
157
(returns the current AC price from the economy table), recalculatePrice (supply-driven recalculation,
called on each coin purchase), updatePriceFromTrade (trade-driven update, called on each matched
trade), applyNaturalFluctuation (called every 15 seconds by a background timer), applyFundingBoost
(called when an asset is fully funded), getPriceHistory (returns time-series price data for charting).
9.11 The Fee Service
The fee service calculates and records all platform fees. The service is implemented in
backend/services/feeService.js and exposes methods: calculateTradingFee (returns 0.1% of trade
amount), calculateCapitalRaiseFee (returns 1% of raised amount), calculateWithdrawalFee (returns 0.5
AC), recordFee (creates a fee_ledger entry and updates economy.total_fees_collected), getTotalFees
(returns the sum of all fees collected). All fees are configurable via system_config.

HIGH_FREQUENCY, DUPLICATE_PATTERN, STRUCTURING, VELOCITY_CHECK — and
returns the list of triggered flags), shouldBlock (returns true if three or more AML flags are triggered
simultaneously, indicating the transaction should be blocked).
9.13 The Document Processor Service
The document processor service handles file uploads for asset verification. The service is implemented

uploads/<assetId>/ with a crypto-randomized filename, computes SHA-256 hash for duplicate
detection, creates an asset_documents entry), checkDuplicate (compares a document hash against all
existing document hashes in the database), getDocuments (returns all documents for an asset),
deleteDocument (removes a document — admin-only).
9.14 The WebSocket Server Service
The WebSocket server service is documented in detail in Chapter 10. It is implemented in
backend/services/wsServer.js and exposes methods: broadcast (sends a message to all subscribers on a

channel), subscribe (registers a client connection on a channel), unsubscribe (removes a client

9.15 Summary
This chapter has documented Averon's service layer: the thirteen domain services, the in-memory event
bus that connects them, and each service's responsibilities and interfaces. The services are the heart of
the platform's business logic — they coordinate state transitions, enforce compliance, manage funds,
and broadcast real-time updates. Chapter 10 dives into the real-time event bus and WebSocket layer in
more detail, documenting the fourteen event types and six subscriber channels that connect the service
layer to clients.
KEY TAKEAWAYS
• 13 domain services, each a singleton initialized at startup, communicate exclusively via the
in-memory event bus.
• Event bus: in-memory pub/sub with 14 event types — chosen over message queues for
simplicity in single-process deployment.
• Asset service owns the 14-state lifecycle state machine; AI pipeline runs 5-stage
verification.
• Trading engine implements price-time priority with maker-price-wins and circuit breaker.
• Payment service implements 8-step minting flow with double-mint prevention.
• Each service has clearly defined responsibility and well-documented interface — modular
and testable.

CHAPTER 24
Real-Time Event Bus & WebSocket
Layer
The in-memory pub/sub singleton with 14 event types and the WebSocket server with 6 channels
that connect services to clients.
Averon is a real-time platform. Asset statuses change, trades execute, prices update,
blocks are mined — all of these events need to reach clients within milliseconds to
provide a responsive user experience. This chapter documents the two layers that
make this possible: the in-memory event bus that services use to communicate with
each other, and the WebSocket server that pushes events to clients.
10.1 The Event Bus
The event bus is Averon's internal nervous system. It is an in-memory pub/sub singleton implemented in
backend/services/eventBus.js. The bus is created once at startup and shared across all services. Any
service can publish an event by calling eventBus.emit(eventType, payload), and any service can
subscribe to an event by calling eventBus.subscribe(eventType, callback). The callback is invoked
synchronously when the event is emitted, with the payload as its argument.
The bus supports fourteen event types, each corresponding to a category of state change on the
platform. The events are: BLOCK_MINED (emitted when a new block is added to the chain),
TRADE_EXECUTED (emitted when an order is matched and a trade executes), ORDER_PLACED
(emitted when a new order is submitted to the book), ORDER_CANCELLED (emitted when an order
is cancelled), PRICE_UPDATED (emitted when the AC price changes — by trade, mint, or natural
fluctuation), ASSET_CREATED (emitted when a new asset is listed), ASSET_STATUS_CHANGED
(emitted when an asset transitions between lifecycle states), ASSET_FUNDED (emitted when an asset
is fully funded), TOKEN_PURCHASED (emitted when an investor buys asset tokens),
COINS_MINTED (emitted when AC is minted from a fiat purchase), USER_REGISTERED (emitted
when a new user registers), NOTIFICATION (generic notification event), ESCROW_LOCKED /
ESCROW_RELEASED (emitted when escrow state changes), and CIRCUIT_BREAKER (emitted
when the trading circuit breaker triggers or resets).

---END---
PAGE_161
AVERON
Rishabh Gupta
AVERON PROTOCOL SERIES  ·  VOL. I
160
Figure 10.1 — Event Bus and WebSocket Channel Topology. 14 event types flow from services through the bus to 6
WebSocket subscriber channels. Services communicate exclusively via the event bus; clients receive events via WebSocket
channels.
10.2 Why an In-Memory Bus, Not a Message Queue
A common architectural question is why Averon uses an in-memory event bus rather than a message
queue like RabbitMQ, Kafka, or Redis Pub/Sub. The answer is that Averon is designed to run as a
single process, and an in-memory bus is sufficient — and significantly simpler — for single-process
coordination.
A message queue provides three capabilities that an in-memory bus does not: persistence (messages
survive process restarts), durability (messages are delivered even if the subscriber is temporarily
unavailable), and cross-process distribution (messages can be delivered to subscribers in different
processes). Averon does not need any of these capabilities for its internal event bus. The events
represent real-time notifications of state changes that are already persisted in the database and on the
blockchain. If the process restarts, the state is recovered from the database and blockchain, and the
events are simply not re-emitted — clients reconnect via WebSocket and receive the current state.
For multi-process deployments (planned in Phase 2), the blockchain itself serves as the coordination
mechanism. Transactions mined into blocks are visible to all processes, and each process can
independently emit events based on the blocks it observes. This is a cleaner architecture than adding a
message queue, because it uses the blockchain — which is already required for the platform's core
functionality — as the message transport.

---END---
PAGE_162
AVERON
Rishabh Gupta
AVERON PROTOCOL SERIES  ·  VOL. I
161
10.3 The WebSocket Server
The WebSocket server is the bridge between the event bus and clients. It is implemented in
backend/services/wsServer.js using the ws package. The server runs on the same port as the HTTP
server (default 4200), with WebSocket upgrade requests handled alongside HTTP requests.
The server supports six channels, each corresponding to a category of real-time data: price
(real-time AC price updates, emitted on PRICE_UPDATED events), trades (executed trade
notifications, emitted on TRADE_EXECUTED events), blocks (new block broadcasts, emitted on
BLOCK_MINED events), assets (asset lifecycle events, emitted on ASSET_CREATED,
ASSET_STATUS_CHANGED, ASSET_FUNDED, TOKEN_PURCHASED events), orders (order
book changes, emitted on ORDER_PLACED and ORDER_CANCELLED events), and users
(user-specific notifications, emitted on NOTIFICATION and CIRCUIT_BREAKER events).
10.4 Client Messages
Clients communicate with the WebSocket server using four message types: subscribe, unsubscribe,
auth, and ping. The subscribe message adds the client to a channel — the client will receive all
subsequent events on that channel. The unsubscribe message removes the client from a channel. The
auth message carries a JWT, which is verified to authenticate the client for user-specific channels (the
'users' channel requires authentication; the other channels are public). The ping message is a keepalive
— the server responds with a pong to confirm the connection is alive.
// Client-side WebSocket example
const ws = new WebSocket("ws://localhost:4200");
ws.onopen = () => {
// Authenticate for user-specific channel
ws.send(JSON.stringify({ type: "auth", token: jwt }));
// Subscribe to public channels
ws.send(JSON.stringify({ type: "subscribe", channel: "price" }));
ws.send(JSON.stringify({ type: "subscribe", channel: "trades" }));
ws.send(JSON.stringify({ type: "subscribe", channel: "blocks" }));
// Subscribe to user-specific channel (requires auth)
ws.send(JSON.stringify({ type: "subscribe", channel: "users" }));
};
ws.onmessage = (event) => {
const msg = JSON.parse(event.data);
console.log(`[${msg.channel}]`, msg.data);
};

---END---
PAGE_163
AVERON
Rishabh Gupta
AVERON PROTOCOL SERIES  ·  VOL. I
162
10.5 Server Broadcasts
The server broadcasts events to subscribers via a simple pattern: when an event is emitted on the event
bus, the WebSocket server checks which channels correspond to that event type, and sends a JSON
message to all subscribers on those channels. The message format is: { channel: 'channelName', data: {
eventType: 'EVENT_TYPE', payload: {...}, timestamp: ... } }.
For user-specific events (NOTIFICATION, CIRCUIT_BREAKER), the server only sends the
message to the specific user's connection, not to all subscribers on the channel. This is enforced by the
auth mechanism — a client must have authenticated via the auth message before it can subscribe to the
'users' channel, and the server tracks which user each authenticated connection belongs to.
10.6 Authentication
Authentication on the WebSocket server is optional but required for user-specific data. A client can
subscribe to public channels (price, trades, blocks, assets, orders) without authentication, but must
authenticate before subscribing to the 'users' channel. Authentication is performed by sending an auth
message with a JWT; the server verifies the JWT using the same HMAC-SHA256 secret used by the
HTTP API, and if valid, attaches the user to the connection.
Authentication is one-way — the server does not send authentication challenges, and the client is
responsible for sending the auth message before subscribing to user-specific channels. If the client
subscribes to the 'users' channel without authenticating, the server responds with an error message and
does not add the client to the channel. JWT expiration is not enforced on existing WebSocket
connections — once authenticated, a connection remains authenticated until it is closed, even if the
JWT expires.
10.7 Connection Lifecycle
WebSocket connections have a simple lifecycle. The client opens the connection with a WebSocket
handshake (HTTP upgrade request with the Upgrade: websocket header). The server accepts the
handshake and the connection is established. The client can then send subscribe, unsubscribe, auth, and
ping messages, and the server can send channel broadcasts and pong responses. The connection remains
open until either side closes it (the client calls ws.close(), or the server terminates the connection due to
inactivity or shutdown).
On server shutdown, the WebSocket server broadcasts a shutdown notification to all connected
clients, then closes all connections. This gives clients the opportunity to display a shutdown message to

---END---
PAGE_164
AVERON
Rishabh Gupta
AVERON PROTOCOL SERIES  ·  VOL. I
163
users and to attempt reconnection (typically with exponential backoff). On reconnection, clients
re-authenticate and re-subscribe to channels — the server does not remember previous subscriptions
across reconnections.
10.8 Use Cases
The real-time layer enables several use cases that would be impossible with HTTP polling. The most
important is live price updates — the AC price changes every 15 seconds (natural fluctuation), on every
trade, and on every coin mint, and traders need to see these changes in real time to make informed
decisions. With WebSocket, price updates reach clients within milliseconds of being emitted; with
HTTP polling, clients would need to poll every second to achieve similar latency, generating significant
server load.
Another important use case is live order book updates. When a new order is placed or an existing
order is cancelled or filled, the order book changes. Traders monitoring the book need to see these
changes immediately to react to market movements. The 'orders' channel pushes ORDER_PLACED
and ORDER_CANCELLED events to subscribers within milliseconds of the underlying action.
Asset lifecycle events are also pushed in real time. When an asset transitions from
documents_uploaded to ai_analyzing, or from active to funding, or from funded to payout_pending,
subscribers on the 'assets' channel receive the transition notification. This is particularly useful for asset
owners monitoring their own assets and for investors monitoring assets they have invested in.
10.9 Summary
This chapter has documented Averon's real-time layer: the in-memory event bus with fourteen event
types, the WebSocket server with six channels, the four client message types (subscribe, unsubscribe,
auth, ping), the broadcast pattern, and the authentication model. The real-time layer is what makes
Averon feel responsive — price updates, trade executions, and asset lifecycle transitions reach clients
within milliseconds of occurring. This concludes Part II of the book, which has covered the system
architecture from the blockchain layer through the real-time layer. Part III dives into the asset
tokenization lifecycle — the fourteen-state state machine that governs every asset on the platform.
KEY TAKEAWAYS
• Event bus: in-memory pub/sub singleton with 14 event types (BLOCK_MINED,
TRADE_EXECUTED, ORDER_PLACED, etc.).

---END---
PAGE_165
AVERON
Rishabh Gupta
AVERON PROTOCOL SERIES  ·  VOL. I
164
• WebSocket server: 6 channels (price, trades, blocks, assets, orders, users) with JWT-based
auth.
• Client messages: subscribe, unsubscribe, auth, ping. Server broadcasts: channel-specific
event messages.
• Authentication optional for public channels, required for 'users' channel.
• In-memory bus (not message queue) is sufficient because events represent real-time
notifications of already-persisted state.
• Multi-process deployments use the blockchain itself as the coordination mechanism.
Discussion Questions
Averon's event bus is in-memory and does not survive process restarts. What events would be most disruptive
to miss, and how would you handle the gap?
The six WebSocket channels are public except 'users.' Why is this distinction important, and what additional
channels might you add?
How would you extend the real-time layer to support mobile push notifications? What new components
would be needed?

PART VI
The Asset Lifecycle
The 14-state state machine, AI verification, and escrow &
settlement flows
AVERON PROTOCOL SERIES

CHAPTER 25
The 14-State Asset Lifecycle
Every asset on Averon moves through this exact state graph; transitions are atomic, audited, and
on-chain.
This is the heart of the book. The fourteen-state asset lifecycle state machine is the
single most important component of the Averon platform. Every asset — whether it
is a piece of real estate, a commodity, a patent, or a carbon credit — moves through
this exact state graph. Understanding the state machine is essential for
understanding Averon.
11.1 Why a State Machine
Asset lifecycle management is fundamentally a state machine problem. An asset moves through discrete
states — draft, verified, active, funded, completed — and the transitions between states are governed by
rules. A state machine makes these rules explicit, deterministic, and auditable. Without a state machine,
asset lifecycle management devolves into ad-hoc flag fields (is_verified, is_active, is_funded,
is_completed) that can be set in any order by any code path, producing inconsistent states that are
difficult to debug and impossible to audit.
Averon's state machine has fourteen states and thirteen transitions. Each state represents a discrete
phase in the asset's life, and each transition represents a specific event that moves the asset from one
phase to the next. Transitions are atomic — they either succeed completely (asset moves to the new
state, audit log entry is created, blockchain transaction is signed and mined, related database tables are
updated) or fail completely (no state change, no audit log entry, no blockchain transaction, no database
updates).

---END---
PAGE_168
AVERON
Rishabh Gupta
AVERON PROTOCOL SERIES  ·  VOL. I
167
Figure 11.1 — The 14-State Asset Lifecycle State Machine. Every asset on Averon moves through this exact state graph.
Transitions are atomic, audited, and on-chain. This is the most important diagram in the book.
11.2 The Fourteen States
The fourteen states are grouped into four phases: creation and verification (draft, documents_uploaded,
ai_analyzing, verified, rejected), compliance and activation (compliance_review, active), funding and
payout (funding, funded, payout_pending, completed), and expiry and refund (expired, refunding,
closed). Each state is described below with its entry conditions, exit conditions, and what the asset can
do while in that state.
State 1: draft
The asset has been created by the owner with title, description, category, raise amount, and listing
duration. No documents have been uploaded, no AI analysis has run, no tokens have been issued. The
asset is invisible to investors. Entry: createAsset API call. Exit: documents_uploaded (after document
upload) or deleted (by owner or admin).
State 2: documents_uploaded
The owner has uploaded 1-10 supporting documents (JPG, PNG, WebP, PDF, max 10 MB each).
Files are stored in uploads/<assetId>/ with crypto-randomized filenames. SHA-256 hashes are

---END---
PAGE_169
AVERON
Rishabh Gupta
AVERON PROTOCOL SERIES  ·  VOL. I
168
computed for duplicate detection. The asset is still invisible to investors. Entry: uploadDocuments API
call. Exit: ai_analyzing (when AI analysis is triggered).
State 3: ai_analyzing
The AI pipeline is running on the asset. The five-stage pipeline (document ingestion, duplicate
check, AI analysis via Gemini 2.0 Flash, fraud detection, tokenization recommendation) executes
synchronously. The asset is still invisible to investors. Entry: analyzeAsset API call. Exit: verified (if AI
verification succeeds) or rejected (if AI verification fails).
State 4: verified
The AI pipeline has completed successfully. The asset has an AI-generated valuation, risk score, risk
level, analysis narrative, concerns, and confidence. Verification criteria: verified equals true AND
confidence greater than 50 AND no critical fraud flags. The asset is still invisible to investors. Entry: AI
pipeline completion with successful verification. Exit: compliance_review (when the owner confirms
tokenization).
State 5: rejected
The AI pipeline has completed but verification failed. Common reasons: confidence below 50,
critical fraud flags triggered (duplicate documents, raise-to-value ratio extreme, category-specific risk
anomalies). The asset is invisible to investors and cannot proceed. Entry: AI pipeline completion with
failed verification. Exit: closed (after admin or owner review) or documents_uploaded (if the owner
re-uploads documents and re-triggers analysis).
State 6: compliance_review
The owner has confirmed tokenization. The compliance service runs checks: raise amount within
limits, minimum documents present, description length meets thresholds. Token count is calculated
(2-10,000, risk-adjusted), token price is calculated (raise_amount / token_count), an escrow account is
created with a unique address, and an ASSET_CREATE transaction is signed by the owner and mined
into a block. The asset is still invisible to investors. Entry: confirmAsset API call (from verified state).
Exit: active (if compliance passes) or rejected (if compliance fails).
State 7: active
The asset is fully tokenized and visible to investors. The asset appears in the marketplace with its
full details (title, description, category, raise amount, token count, token price, AI analysis, documents).
Investors can purchase tokens. The asset remains in this state until the first token is purchased
(transitioning to funding) or the listing duration expires (transitioning to expired). Entry: compliance

---END---
PAGE_170
AVERON
Rishabh Gupta
AVERON PROTOCOL SERIES  ·  VOL. I
169
passes in compliance_review. Exit: funding (on first token purchase) or expired (on listing duration
expiry).
State 8: funding
At least one token has been purchased but not all tokens have been sold. The asset remains visible to
investors. The escrow account holds the funds from investors who have purchased tokens. Entry: first
token purchase. Exit: funded (when all tokens are sold) or expired (on listing duration expiry).
State 9: funded
All tokens have been sold. The asset is no longer visible to investors for primary purchases. The
escrow account holds the full raise amount. The payout process is triggered automatically. Entry: last
token purchase. Exit: payout_pending (when payout process begins).
State 10: payout_pending
The payout process is running. The platform takes a 1% fee (sent to __PLATFORM_FEE__
wallet), the remaining 99% is paid to the asset owner via a PAYOUT transaction on the blockchain, the
AC price is boosted by 2-5% based on the raise amount, and economy stats are updated. Entry:
processFullyFunded function called. Exit: completed (when payout completes).
State 11: completed
The asset has been fully funded and the payout has been completed. The asset owner has received
99% of the raise amount. Token holders own fractional stakes in the asset. The asset remains in this
state indefinitely — there is no automatic transition out of completed. Entry: payout completes. Exit:
closed (if admin or owner closes the asset).
State 12: expired
The listing duration has expired before the asset was fully funded. The asset is no longer visible to
investors for primary purchases. The refund process is triggered automatically. Entry: checkExpiry
function called (every 60 seconds by a background timer) and listing duration has expired. Exit:
refunding (when refund process begins).
State 13: refunding
The refund process is running. Each investor is refunded via a REFUND transaction from the
escrow account to the investor's wallet. Token ownership is cleared (owner_id set back to NULL for all
tokens). Entry: processRefund function called. Exit: closed (when all refunds complete).

---END---
PAGE_171
AVERON
Rishabh Gupta
AVERON PROTOCOL SERIES  ·  VOL. I
170
State 14: closed
The asset has reached a terminal state. This can happen in three ways: (a) the asset was rejected and
the owner or admin has closed it, (b) the asset was completed and the owner or admin has closed it, (c)
the asset expired and all refunds have been processed. Once closed, the asset cannot be reactivated.
Entry: terminal transition from rejected, completed, or refunding. Exit: none — closed is a terminal
state.
11.3 The Thirteen Transitions
Each of the thirteen transitions has a specific trigger, a guard condition, and a set of actions that execute
atomically. The transitions are documented below in the order they typically occur in a successful asset
lifecycle.
draft → documents_uploaded (upload docs)
Triggered when the owner uploads at least one document via the uploadDocuments API. Guard:
asset status equals draft. Actions: store files in uploads/<assetId>/, compute SHA-256 hashes, create
asset_documents entries, update asset status to documents_uploaded, create audit log entry.
documents_uploaded → ai_analyzing (trigger AI)
Triggered when the owner calls the analyzeAsset API. Guard: asset status equals
documents_uploaded, at least one document uploaded. Actions: update asset status to ai_analyzing,
create audit log entry, invoke aiPipeline.analyzeAsset(assetId).
ai_analyzing → verified (verified)
Triggered when the AI pipeline completes with verified equals true AND confidence greater than
50 AND no critical fraud flags. Actions: store AI analysis results in the asset row (ai_verified,
ai_valuation, 
ai_risk_score, 
ai_risk_level, 
ai_confidence, 
ai_analysis, 
ai_concerns), 
create
asset_valuations entry, update asset status to verified, create audit log entry.
ai_analyzing → rejected (rejected)
Triggered when the AI pipeline completes with verified equals false OR confidence less than or
equal to 50 OR critical fraud flags triggered. Actions: store AI analysis results (for transparency),
update asset status to rejected, create audit log entry. The owner can re-upload documents and re-trigger
analysis to attempt verification again.
verified → compliance_review (compliance check)

---END---
PAGE_172
AVERON
Rishabh Gupta
AVERON PROTOCOL SERIES  ·  VOL. I
171
Triggered when the owner calls the confirmAsset API. Guard: asset status equals verified. Actions:
update asset status to compliance_review, run compliance checks (raise amount within limits, minimum
documents present, description length), calculate token count (2-10,000, risk-adjusted), calculate token
price (raise_amount / token_count), create escrow account, sign ASSET_CREATE transaction, mine
transaction into block, create audit log entry.
compliance_review → active (approved)
Triggered when compliance checks pass. Actions: update asset status to active, create asset_tokens
entries (one row per token, all with owner_id = NULL), emit ASSET_CREATED event, create audit
log entry.
active → funding (first purchase)
Triggered when the first token is purchased. Guard: asset status equals active. Actions: investor's
wallet signs INVEST transaction transferring purchase amount to escrow account, mine transaction into
block, atomically claim tokens (UPDATE asset_tokens SET owner_id = ? WHERE id = ? AND
owner_id IS NULL), update asset status to funding, emit TOKEN_PURCHASED and
ASSET_STATUS_CHANGED events, create audit log entry.
funding → funded (fully funded)
Triggered when the last unclaimed token is claimed. Guard: no remaining unclaimed tokens for the
asset. Actions: update asset status to funded, emit ASSET_FUNDED event, create audit log entry,
automatically trigger processFullyFunded.
funded → payout_pending (process payout)
Triggered automatically when the asset enters the funded state. Actions: update asset status to
payout_pending, calculate 1% fee, sign FEE transaction (escrow → __PLATFORM_FEE__), sign
PAYOUT transaction (escrow → owner, 99% of balance), mine both transactions into block, apply AC
price boost (2-5% based on raise amount), update economy stats (total_raised_inr, market_cap), create
audit log entry.
payout_pending → completed (payout complete)
Triggered when the payout process completes successfully. Actions: update asset status to
completed, emit ASSET_STATUS_CHANGED event, create audit log entry. The asset remains in
completed indefinitely.
active → expired (deadline reached)

---END---
PAGE_173
AVERON
Rishabh Gupta
AVERON PROTOCOL SERIES  ·  VOL. I
172
Triggered by the checkExpiry background timer (runs every 60 seconds) when the listing duration
has expired. Guard: asset status equals active or funding. Actions: update asset status to expired, emit
ASSET_STATUS_CHANGED event, create audit log entry, automatically trigger processRefund.
expired → refunding (trigger refund)
Triggered automatically when the asset enters the expired state. Actions: update asset status to
refunding, for each investor who purchased tokens: sign REFUND transaction (escrow → investor),
mine transaction into block, clear token ownership (set owner_id = NULL), update escrow_transactions
with REFUND entry, create audit log entry.
refunding → closed (refunds complete)
Triggered when all refunds have been processed. Actions: update asset status to closed, emit
ASSET_STATUS_CHANGED event, create audit log entry. The asset is now in a terminal state.
11.4 Atomicity Guarantees
Every transition is atomic — it either succeeds completely or fails completely. This is implemented via
database transactions (BEGIN / COMMIT / ROLLBACK) and atomic database operations (UPDATE
WHERE conditions). If any step in a transition fails, the entire transition is rolled back, leaving the asset
in its pre-transition state.
The most critical atomicity guarantee is the token claim operation. When an investor purchases
tokens, the system executes: UPDATE asset_tokens SET owner_id = ? WHERE id = ? AND owner_id
IS NULL. This SQL statement atomically claims an unclaimed token — if another investor claimed the
same token in the interim, the WHERE clause fails (owner_id is no longer NULL), the affected row
count is zero, and the system rolls back the entire purchase and refunds the investor. This prevents the
race condition where two investors could both claim the same token.
11.5 Audit Trail
Every transition creates an entry in the audit_log table. The entry contains: action (describing the
transition), details (JSON with old_status, new_status, asset_id, user_id, tx_hash), prev_hash (hash of
the previous audit log entry), entry_hash (hash of this entry, computed from prev_hash + user_id +
action + resource_type + resource_id + timestamp). The hash chain makes the audit log tamper-evident
— any modification to an entry breaks the chain and is detectable via verifyAuditChain().

---END---
PAGE_174
AVERON
Rishabh Gupta
AVERON PROTOCOL SERIES  ·  VOL. I
173
The audit trail is what makes Averon suitable for institutional use. Traditional asset management
systems rely on database logs that can be modified by anyone with database access, and modifications
are difficult to detect after the fact. Averon's hash-chained audit log provides cryptographic proof of
every state transition, which is essential for regulatory compliance and dispute resolution.
11.6 Summary
This chapter has documented the fourteen-state asset lifecycle state machine — the heart of the Averon
platform. Every asset on Averon moves through this exact state graph, with each transition atomic,
audited, and on-chain. The state machine is what makes Averon a lifecycle management platform rather
than just a tokenization platform. Chapter 12 dives into the AI verification pipeline that drives the
ai_analyzing → verified / rejected transition; Chapter 13 covers the token investment, escrow, and
settlement flows that drive the active → funding → funded → payout_pending → completed transitions.
KEY TAKEAWAYS
• 14 states: draft, documents_uploaded, ai_analyzing, verified, rejected, compliance_review,
active, funding, funded, payout_pending, completed, expired, refunding, closed.
• 13 transitions, each with specific trigger, guard condition, and atomic actions.
• Atomicity via database transactions and UPDATE WHERE conditions — race condition
protection via WHERE owner_id IS NULL.
• Every transition creates an audit log entry with hash-chained entry_hash.
• State machine makes asset lifecycle explicit, deterministic, and auditable — vs ad-hoc flag
fields.
Discussion Questions
The 14-state state machine has 13 transitions. Can you identify a transition that might be missing — a state
change that should be possible but isn't?
If an asset is in the 'funding' state and the owner wants to cancel the raise, what should happen? Design the
transition.
The 'closed' state is terminal. Should there be a 'reopened' state for assets that were closed prematurely?
What are the trade-offs?

CHAPTER 26
AI-Powered Verification & Analysis
The five-stage AI pipeline that evaluates every asset before tokenization is permitted.
Asset verification is the make-or-break step in tokenization. If verification is too lax,
fraudulent or over-valued assets reach investors; if verification is too strict,
legitimate assets are rejected and the platform fails to attract inventory. Averon's AI
pipeline is designed to thread this needle — automated enough to scale, rigorous
enough to trust.
12.1 The Five-Stage Pipeline
The AI pipeline runs five stages in sequence on every submitted asset. The stages are: document
ingestion, duplicate check, AI analysis, fraud detection, and tokenization recommendation. Each stage
produces outputs that feed the next stage, and the final stage produces the structured JSON payload that
drives the compliance review and token count calculation.
Figure 12.1 — AI Verification Pipeline (5 Stages). Every asset passes through this pipeline before tokenization is permitted.
The primary AI engine is Gemini 2.0 Flash; a category-specific fallback engine ensures resilience.

---END---
PAGE_176
AVERON
Rishabh Gupta
AVERON PROTOCOL SERIES  ·  VOL. I
175
12.2 Stage 1: Document Ingestion
The first stage classifies uploaded files by type (JPG, PNG, WebP, PDF) and computes a quality score
based on document count, file sizes, and types. The quality score is a 0-100 integer that reflects how
complete and high-quality the document set is. A document set with five high-resolution PDFs scores
higher than a set with two low-resolution JPGs, for example. The quality score feeds into later stages —
a low quality score reduces the AI's confidence in its valuation, and a very low quality score can trigger
a fraud flag.
Files are stored in uploads/<assetId>/ with crypto-randomized filenames to prevent path traversal
attacks. The crypto.randomBytes(16).toString('hex') function generates a 32-character hexadecimal
filename that is virtually impossible to guess, and the original filename is stored in the asset_documents
table for display purposes. SHA-256 hashes are computed for each file and stored for the next stage's
duplicate check.
12.3 Stage 2: Duplicate Check
The second stage compares each document's SHA-256 hash against all existing document hashes in the
database. If a hash matches an existing document — meaning the exact same file has been uploaded
before, possibly for a different asset — the document is flagged as a potential duplicate. Duplicate
documents are a strong signal of fraud: a malicious user might try to tokenize the same asset multiple
times, or might try to reuse documents from a legitimate asset to support a fraudulent one.
Duplicate detection is a critical fraud prevention mechanism, but it is not perfect. A sophisticated
attacker could modify a document slightly (changing a single byte) to produce a different hash, evading
duplicate detection. The AI analysis stage (Stage 3) provides a second layer of defense by analyzing the
document content semantically — even if the hash differs, the AI can recognize that two documents are
photos of the same physical asset.
12.4 Stage 3: AI Analysis
The third stage is the core of the pipeline: a multimodal call to Google Gemini 2.0 Flash. The request
includes the asset's metadata (title, description, category, raise amount) and the document images (for
image documents) or extracted text (for PDF documents). The model is prompted to analyze the asset
and return a JSON response with the following fields: verified (boolean), estimated_value (decimal),
risk_score (integer 0-100), risk_level (one of 'low', 'medium', 'high', 'critical'), analysis (text narrative),
concerns (array of strings), confidence (decimal 0-100).

---END---
PAGE_177
AVERON
Rishabh Gupta
AVERON PROTOCOL SERIES  ·  VOL. I
176
// Gemini request payload (simplified)
{
"model": "gemini-2.0-flash",
"contents": [{
"parts": [
{ "text": "Analyze this asset..." },
{ "inline_data": { "mime_type": "image/jpeg", "data": "base64..." } },
...
]
}],
"generation_config": {
"response_mime_type": "application/json",
"response_schema": { ... }
}
}
The prompt instructs the model to act as a professional asset valuer, to analyze the documents for
evidence of the asset's existence, ownership, and value, to identify any red flags or inconsistencies, and
to provide a structured assessment. The model's response is parsed as JSON and validated against the
expected schema. If the response is invalid (missing fields, wrong types), the pipeline falls back to the
category-specific engine described below.
12.5 The Fallback Engine
If Gemini is unavailable — due to network error, rate limit, API key issue, or invalid response — the
pipeline falls back to a category-specific engine. This engine uses hardcoded valuation profiles for each
of the twelve asset categories, with randomized adjustments for document quality and raise-to-value
ratio.
For example, the Land category profile specifies an average value of less than 5 lakh rupees and a
risk range of 10-35%. When the fallback engine is invoked for a Land asset, it generates a randomized
valuation within this range, adjusts the risk score based on the document quality score (higher quality =
lower risk), and adjusts the confidence based on the raise-to-value ratio (raise amount close to estimated
value = higher confidence). The fallback engine is intentionally conservative — it tends to produce
lower confidence scores than Gemini, which means assets verified via the fallback are more likely to be
rejected by the verification criteria.
12.6 Stage 4: Fraud Detection
The fourth stage evaluates the AI analysis output for fraud signals. Four critical fraud flags are checked:
duplicate documents (flagged in Stage 2), raise-to-value ratio extreme (raise amount significantly higher

---END---
PAGE_178
AVERON
Rishabh Gupta
AVERON PROTOCOL SERIES  ·  VOL. I
177
than estimated value), low confidence (confidence below 50), and extreme risk score (risk_score above
80). Any of these flags triggers a critical fraud alert, which automatically fails verification regardless of
the AI's verified field.
The fraud detection stage also evaluates non-critical concerns. If the AI's concerns array contains
certain keywords ('inconsistent', 'mismatch', 'unverifiable', 'suspicious'), the asset is flagged for manual
review by an admin. Manual review does not block the asset — the admin can override the AI's decision
and approve the asset — but it adds a human checkpoint for borderline cases.
12.7 Stage 5: Tokenization Recommendation
The fifth and final stage produces the tokenization recommendation: a suggested token count and token
price. The token count is calculated as a function of the raise amount and the AI-assessed risk score.
Higher raise amounts and lower risk scores produce higher token counts (more fractional ownership,
broader investor base). The token count is bounded at 2-10,000 to ensure both single-investor assets (2
tokens) and mass-market assets (10,000 tokens) are supported.
The token price is calculated as raise_amount / token_count, denominated in both INR and AVR
coin. The AVR denomination is computed at the current AC price from the economy table. Both
denominations are stored in the asset row for display purposes; the INR denomination is the canonical
price for compliance and audit, and the AVR denomination is what investors actually pay.
12.8 Verification Criteria
Verification succeeds only if all three of the following conditions are met: verified equals true (from the
AI analysis), confidence is greater than 50, and no critical fraud flags are triggered. If any condition
fails, the asset transitions to the rejected state. The owner can re-upload documents and re-trigger
analysis to attempt verification again.
VERIFICATION CRITERIA (ALL THREE REQUIRED)
1. AI verified = true (Gemini confirms asset existence, ownership, and value)
2. Confidence > 50 (model is sufficiently confident in its assessment)
3. No critical fraud flags (no duplicate documents, no extreme raise-to-value ratio, no extreme risk
score)
12.9 Why Multimodal AI Matters

---END---
PAGE_179
AVERON
Rishabh Gupta
AVERON PROTOCOL SERIES  ·  VOL. I
178
Asset verification is fundamentally a multimodal problem. A real estate valuation requires analyzing
property photos (visual), title deeds (text), and survey documents (structured data). A commodity
valuation requires analyzing warehouse receipts (text) and quality certificates (text + images). A patent
valuation requires analyzing the patent document (text) and any diagrams (images). Text-only AI
models cannot perform these verifications — they cannot see the property photos, cannot read the
warehouse receipts, cannot interpret the patent diagrams.
Gemini 2.0 Flash is a multimodal model that can process text and images together, which is essential
for asset verification. The model can read a title deed, examine the property photos, cross-reference the
two, and produce a coherent assessment. This is a capability that was not available in production AI
systems until recently, and it is what makes automated asset verification practical for the first time.
12.10 Limitations and Future Improvements
The current AI pipeline has several limitations that future versions will address. First, the pipeline runs
only once per asset, at verification time. Continuous monitoring — re-analyzing the asset periodically
throughout its lifecycle to detect changes in valuation or risk — is planned for Phase 2. Second, the
pipeline does not currently incorporate external data sources (market prices, comparable sales,
regulatory filings); the Oracle Layer (planned for Phase 2) will provide this data. Third, the fallback
engine is intentionally conservative, which means some legitimate assets may be rejected when Gemini
is unavailable; future versions will improve the fallback's accuracy.
12.11 Summary
This chapter has documented the five-stage AI verification pipeline: document ingestion, duplicate
check, AI analysis (via Gemini 2.0 Flash with category-specific fallback), fraud detection, and
tokenization recommendation. The pipeline is the gatekeeper of the Averon platform — only assets that
pass verification can be tokenized. Chapter 13 covers what happens after verification: the token
investment, escrow, and settlement flows that move assets from active through funded to completed.
KEY TAKEAWAYS
• 5-stage AI pipeline: document ingestion → duplicate check → AI analysis → fraud
detection → tokenization recommendation.
• Primary AI engine: Google Gemini 2.0 Flash (multimodal: text + images).
• Fallback engine: category-specific profiles (e.g., Land: avg <5L, risk 10-35%) for
resilience when Gemini is unavailable.

---END---
PAGE_180
AVERON
Rishabh Gupta
AVERON PROTOCOL SERIES  ·  VOL. I
179
• Verification criteria: verified=true AND confidence>50 AND no critical fraud flags.
• Multimodal AI is essential — text-only models cannot analyze property photos, warehouse
receipts, or patent diagrams.
• Token count calculated from raise amount and risk score; token price = raise_amount /
token_count.
Discussion Questions
The AI pipeline runs once at verification time. What would continuous monitoring look like, and what events
would trigger re-analysis?
The fallback engine is intentionally conservative. How would you design a more accurate fallback without
relying on Gemini?
Verification criteria require confidence > 50. What would be the impact of raising this threshold to 70?
Lowering it to 30?
