CHAPTER 27
Token Investment, Escrow & Settlement
How investor funds move atomically from buyer wallet to escrow to asset owner, with race
condition protection and full refund on failure.
Once an asset is active, investors can purchase fractional tokens. The investment
flow is the most security-critical flow in the platform — it moves real value between
investors, escrow accounts, and asset owners, and any failure could result in lost
funds or double-spending. This chapter documents the flow in detail, including the
race condition protection that makes it safe under concurrent load.
13.1 The Investment Flow
When an investor purchases tokens, the system executes a five-step flow: balance check, INVEST
transaction signing, atomic token claim, audit log entry, and event emission. Each step is documented
below.
Figure 13.1 — Token Investment Escrow Flow. Investor funds move atomically from buyer wallet to escrow to asset
owner. Race condition protection via UPDATE WHERE owner_id IS NULL ensures no double-claiming of tokens.
Step 1: Balance Check

---END---
PAGE_182
AVERON
Rishabh Gupta
AVERON PROTOCOL SERIES  ·  VOL. I
181
The system checks that the investor's wallet has sufficient AC balance to cover the purchase. The
balance is computed via the UTXO-style scan described in Chapter 6: sum of all transaction outputs to
the wallet, minus all transaction inputs, minus pending outgoing transactions. If the balance is
insufficient, the purchase is rejected with an error. The balance check is not atomic with the rest of the
flow — between the check and the actual transfer, another transaction could reduce the balance — but
the blockchain's signature verification will reject the INVEST transaction if the wallet does not have
sufficient funds at mining time.
Step 2: INVEST Transaction Signing
The investor's wallet signs an INVEST transaction transferring the purchase amount (token_count
times token_price, in AC) from the investor's wallet to the asset's escrow account. The transaction is
signed using ECDSA secp256k1, the same curve used by Bitcoin and Ethereum. The signed transaction
is added to the mempool and mined into the next block.
Step 3: Atomic Token Claim
After the INVEST transaction is mined, the system atomically claims the requested number of
tokens. The claim is executed via the SQL statement: UPDATE asset_tokens SET owner_id = ?,
tx_hash = ?, claimed_at = NOW() WHERE id IN (?) AND owner_id IS NULL. The WHERE owner_id
IS NULL clause ensures that only unclaimed tokens can be claimed — if another investor claimed some
of the same tokens in the interim, those tokens' owner_id is no longer NULL, and the UPDATE fails
for them.
The system checks the affected row count after the UPDATE. If the affected row count is less than
the requested token count (meaning some tokens were claimed by another investor in the interim), the
entire purchase is rolled back: a REFUND transaction is signed transferring the purchase amount back
from the escrow account to the investor's wallet, the token claims are reverted (the UPDATE is rolled
back via the database transaction), and the investor is notified that the purchase failed due to concurrent
activity. The investor can retry the purchase with the remaining available tokens.
Step 4: Audit Log Entry
An audit log entry is created with: action = 'TOKEN_PURCHASED', details = JSON with asset_id,
investor_id, token_count, purchase_amount, tx_hash, prev_hash (hash of the previous audit log entry),
entry_hash (hash of this entry, computed from prev_hash + user_id + action + resource_type +
resource_id + timestamp). The hash chain ensures the audit log is tamper-evident.
Step 5: Event Emission

---END---
PAGE_183
AVERON
Rishabh Gupta
AVERON PROTOCOL SERIES  ·  VOL. I
182
A TOKEN_PURCHASED event is emitted on the event bus. The WebSocket server pushes the
event to subscribers on the 'assets' channel. If this is the first token purchase for the asset, an
ASSET_STATUS_CHANGED event is also emitted (asset transitions from active to funding). If this is
the last token purchase (all tokens are now claimed), an ASSET_FUNDED event is emitted (asset
transitions from funding to funded).
13.2 The Payout Flow
When all tokens are sold, the asset transitions to funded, which automatically triggers the payout flow.
The payout flow releases the escrow balance to the asset owner, takes a 1% platform fee, boosts the AC
price, and updates economy stats. The flow has six steps.
Figure 13.2 — Payout & Settlement Process. Triggered when all asset tokens are sold (funded state), runs in 6 atomic steps:
trigger detection, fee calculation, payout tx signing, block mining, DB updates, price boost.
Step 1: Trigger Detection
The system detects that the asset is fully funded by querying the asset_tokens table: SELECT
COUNT(*) FROM asset_tokens WHERE asset_id = ? AND owner_id IS NULL. If the count is zero,
all tokens are claimed. The asset status is updated to funded, and the processFullyFunded function is
called.
Step 2: Fee Calculation

---END---
PAGE_184
AVERON
Rishabh Gupta
AVERON PROTOCOL SERIES  ·  VOL. I
183
The platform fee is calculated as 1% of the total escrow balance. The fee is recorded in the
fee_ledger table with fee_type = 'capital_raise', amount, reference_id (the asset id), and user_id (the
asset owner). The fee is added to economy.total_fees_collected.
Step 3: Payout Transaction Signing
The asset owner's wallet signs a PAYOUT transaction transferring 99% of the escrow balance to the
owner's wallet. Simultaneously, a FEE transaction is signed transferring 1% to the
__PLATFORM_FEE__ wallet. Both transactions are added to the mempool.
Step 4: Block Mining
The PAYOUT and FEE transactions are mined into the next block. The Merkle root is updated to
include both transaction hashes. The block is added to the chain, and a BLOCK_MINED event is
emitted.
Step 5: Database Updates
The escrow_accounts table is updated: balance is set to 0, total_released is incremented by the
payout amount. The asset owner's wallet balance is incremented by the payout amount (this is computed
from the blockchain, not stored separately). The economy table is updated: total_raised_inr is
incremented by the raise amount, market_cap is recalculated.
Step 6: Price Boost
The AC price is boosted by 2-5% based on the raise amount. The boost formula is: boost = min(5%,
2% + raiseAmount / 1M × 3%). For a raise amount of 1 million rupees, the boost is 2% + 3% = 5%
(capped). For a raise amount of 100,000 rupees, the boost is 2% + 0.3% = 2.3%. The boost is applied to
the current AC price, and a PRICE_UPDATED event is emitted. The boost reflects the positive
economic signal of a successful capital raise — more assets on the platform means more demand for
AC, which justifies a higher price.
13.3 The Refund Flow
If the listing duration expires before the asset is fully funded, the asset transitions to expired, which
automatically triggers the refund flow. The refund flow returns all investor funds from the escrow
account back to the investors, clears token ownership, and closes the asset. The flow has four steps.
Step 1: Expiry Detection

---END---
PAGE_185
AVERON
Rishabh Gupta
AVERON PROTOCOL SERIES  ·  VOL. I
184
A background timer runs every 60 seconds and calls the checkExpiry function. This function
queries all assets with status 'active' or 'funding' and checks if their listing duration has expired:
SELECT * FROM assets WHERE status IN ('active', 'funding') AND created_at + listing_duration *
INTERVAL '1 day' < NOW(). For each expired asset, the status is updated to 'expired' and
processRefund is called.
Step 2: Per-Investor Refund
For each investor who purchased tokens, the system signs a REFUND transaction transferring the
investor's purchase amount back from the escrow account to the investor's wallet. The transaction is
mined into the next block. An escrow_transactions entry is created with type = 'REFUND', user_id,
amount, tx_hash.
Step 3: Token Ownership Clearing
All token ownership is cleared: UPDATE asset_tokens SET owner_id = NULL, tx_hash = NULL,
claimed_at = NULL WHERE asset_id = ?. This returns all tokens to the unclaimed state. If the asset is
later re-listed (after the owner addresses whatever issue caused the raise to fail), the tokens can be
purchased again by new investors.
Step 4: Asset Closure
The asset status is updated to 'closed', and the escrow account is marked as closed (closed_at =
NOW()). An ASSET_STATUS_CHANGED event is emitted. The asset is now in a terminal state and
cannot be reactivated (without admin intervention and a new listing).
13.4 Race Condition Protection in Detail
The most critical safety mechanism in the investment flow is the race condition protection for token
claims. Without this protection, two concurrent investors could both claim the same token, resulting in
double-spending — both investors would believe they own the token, but the platform only has one
token to give.
The protection is implemented via the SQL statement: UPDATE asset_tokens SET owner_id = ?
WHERE id = ? AND owner_id IS NULL. The WHERE owner_id IS NULL clause is the key — it
ensures that only unclaimed tokens can be claimed. If two concurrent UPDATE statements target the
same token, only one will succeed (the first one to execute); the other will find that owner_id is no
longer NULL and will affect zero rows.

---END---
PAGE_186
AVERON
Rishabh Gupta
AVERON PROTOCOL SERIES  ·  VOL. I
185
The system checks the affected row count after the UPDATE. If the count is less than the requested
token count, some tokens were claimed by another investor in the interim. The entire purchase is then
rolled back: the INVEST transaction is reversed (via a REFUND transaction), the token claims are
reverted (the database transaction is rolled back), and the investor is notified that the purchase failed.
This protection is implemented at the database level, which is the only place it can be truly atomic.
Application-level locking (mutexes, semaphores) would not work across multiple application instances,
and blockchain-level locking would be too slow (block times are 30 seconds). Database-level atomic
operations are the right level of abstraction for this kind of safety guarantee.
13.5 The Trust Model
The escrow model is the trust mechanism of the asset lifecycle. Investor funds are held in escrow — not
by the asset owner — until the raise succeeds. This eliminates the counterparty risk that would
otherwise exist between investors and asset owners.
Without escrow, an asset owner could collect investor funds and then disappear without delivering
the asset. With escrow, the asset owner receives nothing until the raise is fully funded — at which point
the payout is automatic and immediate. This aligns the owner's incentives with the investors' interests:
the owner must convince enough investors to participate, and cannot abscond with partial funds.
The escrow model also protects investors in the failure case. If the raise fails (expiry before full
funding), all investors are refunded automatically. There is no need for investors to request refunds or
for the platform to manually process them — the refund flow runs automatically when the asset expires,
and each investor receives their funds back via a blockchain REFUND transaction that is visible
on-chain and verifiable.
13.6 Summary
This chapter has documented the token investment, escrow, and settlement flows: the five-step
investment flow (balance check, INVEST transaction signing, atomic token claim, audit log entry, event
emission), the six-step payout flow (trigger detection, fee calculation, payout transaction signing, block
mining, database updates, price boost), the four-step refund flow (expiry detection, per-investor refund,
token ownership clearing, asset closure), and the race condition protection that makes the investment
flow safe under concurrent load. This concludes Part III of the book. Part IV covers the financial
infrastructure — AVR coin economics, trading engine, and payment gateways.

---END---
PAGE_187
AVERON
Rishabh Gupta
AVERON PROTOCOL SERIES  ·  VOL. I
186
KEY TAKEAWAYS
• 5-step investment flow: balance check → INVEST tx signing → atomic token claim → audit
log → event emission.
• Race condition protection: UPDATE asset_tokens SET owner_id=? WHERE id=? AND
owner_id IS NULL.
• 6-step payout flow: trigger → fee calc → PAYOUT tx → mine → DB updates → price
boost.
• 4-step refund flow on expiry: detect → per-investor REFUND tx → clear token ownership
→ close asset.
• Escrow model eliminates counterparty risk: investor funds held by protocol, not asset
owner, until raise succeeds.
• Failed claims roll back atomically — investor refunded via blockchain REFUND
transaction.
Discussion Questions
The race condition protection uses UPDATE WHERE owner_id IS NULL. What other database-level
patterns could achieve the same safety?
The payout flow takes a 1% fee. How would you design a tiered fee structure that incentivizes larger raises
without excluding small ones?
The refund flow clears token ownership. Should investors who were refunded have priority if the asset is
re-listed? Why or why not?

CHAPTER 28
AVR Coin Economics & Price Engine
The seven utility sinks that create continuous AVR demand, and the four-mechanism price engine
that discovers the AC price.
AVR is the native utility coin of the Averon Protocol. Unlike speculative tokens
whose value depends on market sentiment, AVR's value is designed to come from
platform usage. This chapter documents the seven utility sinks that create continuous
demand for AVR, and the four-mechanism price engine that discovers the AC price.
14.1 The Seven Utility Sinks
AVR serves seven utility functions on the Averon platform. Each function creates demand for AVR —
when users perform the function, they must hold and spend AVR. The seven functions are designed to
scale with platform activity, so as Averon grows, AVR demand grows proportionally.
Figure 14.1 — AVR Coin Utility & Velocity. Seven utility sinks create continuous demand for AVR across the platform
economy. The value driver is usage, not speculation.

---END---
PAGE_190
AVERON
Rishabh Gupta
AVERON PROTOCOL SERIES  ·  VOL. I
189
1. Transaction Fees
Every on-chain transaction consumes AVR as a fee. Minting, transferring, investing, payouts,
refunds, and trades all incur fees. The fee structure is: 0.1% per trade (both sides), 1% capital raise fee
(deducted from escrow payout), 0.5 AC withdrawal fee. All fees are sent to the __PLATFORM_FEE__
wallet and recorded in the fee_ledger table. Transaction fees are the most direct form of AVR demand
— every transaction on the platform burns AVR (in the sense that the fee goes to the platform treasury,
which can burn or redistribute it).
2. Staking
Validators in the Averon network (planned for Phase 2) must stake AVR as collateral. The stake is
slashable — if the validator misbehaves (signs invalid blocks, double-signs, goes offline), a portion of
the stake is burned. Staking creates a large, persistent demand for AVR — validators must acquire and
hold AVR to participate in consensus, and the more validators there are, the more AVR is staked.
3. Governance
AVR holders can vote on protocol parameter changes, fee adjustments, and roadmap priorities.
Voting weight is proportional to AVR holdings. Governance creates demand for AVR among users who
want to influence the protocol's direction — institutional users in particular may accumulate AVR to
ensure their interests are represented in governance decisions.
4. Oracle Services
The Oracle Layer (planned for Phase 2) brings trusted external data onto the chain — prices,
weather, IoT sensors, legal records, market information. Oracle operators are paid in AVR for
providing this data. Smart contracts that consume oracle data pay for the data in AVR. This creates a
market for external data priced in AVR, generating demand from smart contract developers who need
reliable external data.
5. AI Services
The AI Layer provides valuation, risk scoring, and fraud detection as a service. Users who want
on-demand AI analysis (beyond the automatic verification pipeline) pay for it in AVR. This creates
demand from institutional users who need to analyze large portfolios of assets and are willing to pay for
AI-driven insights.
6. Developer Infrastructure
The Developer Platform provides APIs, SDKs, and smart contract tools. Heavy API usage (above
the free tier) is billed in AVR. Smart contract deployment on the AVM requires AVR for deployment

---END---
PAGE_191
AVERON
Rishabh Gupta
AVERON PROTOCOL SERIES  ·  VOL. I
190
fees. This creates demand from developers building on Averon, particularly enterprise developers
integrating Averon into their internal systems.
7. Ecosystem Incentives
AVR is used for ecosystem incentives: mining rewards (0.1 AC per block), liquidity mining
(planned for Phase 2), and grant programs for developers building on Averon. These incentives
distribute AVR to participants who contribute to the platform's growth, aligning their interests with the
platform's success.
14.2 The Value Driver
The seven utility sinks above create continuous demand for AVR that scales with platform activity.
More assets on the platform means more transactions, which means more transaction fees. More
applications means more API calls, which means more developer infrastructure fees. More smart
contracts means more oracle data consumption and more AVM deployment fees. The demand for AVR
is a function of platform usage, not market sentiment.
This is a critical design choice. Many token projects have failed because their tokens had no utility
beyond speculation, leading to boom-and-bust cycles that destroyed platform economics. AVR's seven
utility sinks ensure that the token has fundamental value derived from the platform's activity.
Speculators may still trade AVR, but the underlying demand from utility provides a price floor that pure
speculative tokens lack.
14.3 The Four-Mechanism Price Engine
The AC (Averon Coin) price is driven by four parallel mechanisms that respond to different market
signals. The mechanisms run independently but feed the same single source of truth: the price field in
the economy table.

---END---
PAGE_192
AVERON
Rishabh Gupta
AVERON PROTOCOL SERIES  ·  VOL. I
191
Figure 14.2 — AC Price Engine: Four Mechanisms. The AVR Coin price is driven by four parallel mechanisms:
supply-driven recalculation, trade-driven, natural fluctuation, and asset funding boost.
Mechanism 1: Supply-Driven Recalculation
Triggered on each coin purchase (when AC is minted from fiat). The formula is: price = 1.0 × (1 +
totalSupply / 10000) × (1 + assetsFunded × 0.04). The price increases with circulating supply (more
coins in circulation = higher price, reflecting increased platform adoption) and with successfully funded
assets (more successful raises = higher price, reflecting increased platform utility).
The choice of 10000 as the divisor for totalSupply means that the supply effect becomes significant
only when supply is large — at 10,000 AC, the supply multiplier is 2.0 (price doubles); at 100,000 AC,
the multiplier is 11.0 (price increases 11x). The choice of 0.04 as the per-asset-funded multiplier means
that each funded asset boosts the price by 4% — 25 funded assets double the price through this
mechanism alone.
Mechanism 2: Trade-Driven
Triggered on each matched trade on the AVR exchange. The price is set to the last trade price. This
is direct market price discovery — the order book determines the fair value of AC in real time.
Trade-driven price updates can override supply-driven recalculation (if the trade price is lower) or
reinforce it (if the trade price is higher).
Mechanism 3: Natural Fluctuation
Runs every 15 seconds by a background timer. The formula is: swing = random(-0.25%, +0.25%);
newPrice = max(0.001, currentPrice × (1 + swing)). This simulates organic market movement,

---END---
PAGE_193
AVERON
Rishabh Gupta
AVERON PROTOCOL SERIES  ·  VOL. I
192
providing a sense of liveliness even when no trades are occurring. The swing is bounded at ±0.25% to
prevent excessive volatility, and the floor of 0.001 AC prevents the price from collapsing to zero.
Mechanism 4: Asset Funding Boost
Triggered when an asset is fully funded. The formula is: boost = min(5%, 2% + raiseAmount / 1M ×
3%). The boost is 2% plus up to 3% based on the raise amount, capped at 5%. For a raise amount of 1
million rupees, the boost is 5% (the cap). For smaller raises, the boost is proportionally smaller. The
boost reflects the positive economic signal of a successful capital raise.
14.4 Price History and Charting
All price changes are persisted to the price_history table for charting and historical analysis. Each row
contains: price, volume (sum of trade amounts in the period), high, low, open, close, recorded_at. The
/api/economy/price-history endpoint returns time-series price data for charting, with configurable
resolution (1-minute, 5-minute, 1-hour, 1-day candles).
Price history is also used by the circuit breaker (Chapter 15) to detect excessive price movements.
The circuit breaker queries the price_history table for the last 60 minutes of price data, computes the
percentage change from the first price to the last price, and if the change exceeds 10% (configurable),
halts all trading.
14.5 Minting and Burning
AVR is minted when users purchase it with fiat currency, and burned when users withdraw it back to
fiat. The minting and burning flows are documented in detail in Chapter 16, but the economic impact is
worth noting here. Minting increases circulating supply (and triggers supply-driven price recalculation);
burning decreases circulating supply (and does not directly affect price, but reduces the supply
multiplier in the next recalculation).
The total supply of AVR is not capped — it expands when users purchase it with fiat and contracts
when users withdraw it. This design is intentional: it ties the AVR supply to actual demand for the
platform's services. If demand grows, supply grows to accommodate it; if demand shrinks, supply
shrinks. A capped supply (like Bitcoin's 21 million) would create deflationary pressure that could
discourage usage — users would hoard AVR rather than spend it on transaction fees, undermining the
utility sinks that drive the platform's economics.
14.6 Summary

---END---
PAGE_194
AVERON
Rishabh Gupta
AVERON PROTOCOL SERIES  ·  VOL. I
193
This chapter has documented AVR coin economics and the price engine: the seven utility sinks
(transaction fees, staking, governance, oracle services, AI services, developer infrastructure, ecosystem
incentives) that create continuous demand, the four-mechanism price engine (supply-driven
recalculation, trade-driven, natural fluctuation, asset funding boost) that discovers the AC price, the
minting and burning flows that tie supply to demand, and the price history table that supports charting
and circuit breaker detection. Chapter 15 covers the trading engine that executes trades on the AVR
exchange.
KEY TAKEAWAYS
• 7 utility sinks: transaction fees, staking, governance, oracle services, AI services, developer
infrastructure, ecosystem incentives.
• Value driver: usage, not speculation. More assets → more txs → more AVR demand.
• 4-mechanism price engine: supply-driven recalculation, trade-driven, natural fluctuation
(every 15s), asset funding boost.
• Supply formula: price = 1.0 × (1 + totalSupply/10000) × (1 + assetsFunded × 0.04).
• Funding boost: min(5%, 2% + raiseAmount/1M × 3%) — successful raises boost AC price.
• AVR supply is uncapped — expands with fiat purchases, contracts with withdrawals, tied
to actual demand.
Discussion Questions
AVR has seven utility sinks. Which sink do you think will generate the most demand at scale, and why?
The four-mechanism price engine can produce conflicting signals. How should the engine resolve conflicts
between supply-driven and trade-driven prices?
AVR supply is uncapped. What are the long-term implications of this choice for AVR's role as a store of
value vs. a medium of exchange?

---END---

AVERON
Rishabh Gupta
AVERON PROTOCOL SERIES  ·  VOL. I

CHAPTER 29
Trading Engine & Marketplace
Order book matching with price-time priority, maker-price-wins semantics, and a circuit breaker
that halts trading on excessive price moves.
Averon includes an integrated order-book exchange for trading AVR coins. The
trading engine matches buy and sell orders using price-time priority with
maker-price-wins semantics, supports partial fills and market orders, and includes a
circuit breaker that halts trading when prices move too quickly. This chapter
documents the engine in detail.
15.1 Order Book Matching Rules
The trading engine matches orders using four rules. Price-Time Priority: the best price is matched first
(highest bid, lowest ask), and within the same price, the earliest order is matched first. Maker Price
Wins: when a buy order matches a sell order, the sell (maker) order's price is the trade price — the
maker always gets their price. No Self-Trade: buy.user_id equals sell.user_id is skipped, preventing
users from trading with themselves to manipulate volume. Partial Fills: orders can be filled across
multiple matches, with the remaining quantity staying on the book.

---END---
PAGE_196
AVERON
Rishabh Gupta
AVERON PROTOCOL SERIES  ·  VOL. I
195
Figure 15.1 — Order Book Matching Engine. Price-Time Priority with maker-price-wins semantics; partial fills supported.
The spread between best bid and best ask is the market maker's profit opportunity.
15.2 Order Types
The trading engine supports two order types: limit orders and market orders. A limit order specifies a
price and quantity — the order is added to the book at the specified price and matched only against
orders on the opposite side at that price or better. A market order specifies only a quantity — the order
is matched immediately against the best available prices on the opposite side, walking the book until the
quantity is filled or the book is exhausted.
Limit orders that cannot be immediately filled (or can only be partially filled) remain on the book
until they are filled, cancelled by the user, or expired (orders do not currently expire automatically —
this is planned for a future release). Market orders that cannot be fully filled (because the book is
exhausted) are filled with whatever quantity is available, and the remainder is cancelled. Market orders
never rest on the book — they either fill immediately or are cancelled.
15.3 The Matching Algorithm
When a new order is submitted, the matching algorithm attempts to match it against existing orders on
the opposite side of the book. The algorithm is:

---END---
PAGE_197
AVERON
Rishabh Gupta
AVERON PROTOCOL SERIES  ·  VOL. I
196
function matchOrder(newOrder):
while newOrder.remaining > 0:
bestOpposite = getBestOrder(oppositeSide(newOrder.side))
if bestOpposite is None: break # no more orders to match
# No self-trade
if newOrder.user_id == bestOpposite.user_id:
skip bestOpposite and continue
# Check price compatibility
if newOrder.side == "buy" and newOrder.price < bestOpposite.price:
break # buy price too low
if newOrder.side == "sell" and newOrder.price > bestOpposite.price:
break # sell price too high
# Determine trade price and quantity
tradePrice = bestOpposite.price # maker price wins
tradeQty = min(newOrder.remaining, bestOpposite.remaining)
# Execute trade
executeTrade(newOrder, bestOpposite, tradePrice, tradeQty)
# Update remaining quantities
newOrder.remaining -= tradeQty
bestOpposite.remaining -= tradeQty
if bestOpposite.remaining == 0: remove from book
if newOrder.remaining > 0 and newOrder.type == "limit":
add newOrder to book
15.4 Trade Execution
When a trade is executed, the system performs five actions atomically. Trade record: a coin_trades
entry is created with buy_order_id, sell_order_id, amount, price, buyer_fee, seller_fee, tx_hash. Order
updates: the buy and sell orders' filled and remaining quantities are updated; if remaining is zero, the
order status is set to 'filled', otherwise 'partial'. Blockchain transaction: a TRADE transaction is signed
transferring AC from the buyer's wallet to the seller's wallet. Fee collection: 0.1% of the trade amount
is collected as a trading fee from both buyer and seller, sent via FEE transactions to the
__PLATFORM_FEE__ wallet. Price update: the AC price is updated to the trade price (Mechanism 2
of the price engine).
15.5 The Circuit Breaker

---END---
PAGE_198
AVERON
Rishabh Gupta
AVERON PROTOCOL SERIES  ·  VOL. I
197
The circuit breaker is a safety mechanism that halts all trading when the AC price moves more than
10% within any one-hour window. The circuit breaker prevents cascading liquidations and panic selling
during extreme market movements, giving the market time to stabilize. Both the threshold (10%) and
the window (1 hour) are configurable via system_config.
Figure 15.2 — Circuit Breaker Logic. Trading halts automatically when price moves more than 10% within any 1-hour
window. The threshold and window are configurable via admin endpoint.
The circuit breaker triggers on every trade execution. After a trade updates the AC price, the system
queries the price_history table for the last 60 minutes of price data, computes the percentage change
from the first price to the last price, and if the change exceeds 10%, halts trading. The halt is
implemented by setting a flag (circuit_breaker_active) in the system_config table. While the flag is
true, all new order submissions are rejected with an error message.
The circuit breaker auto-resets when the 60-minute window no longer contains a 10%+ price move.
This happens naturally as time passes and the offending price moves out of the window. When the
circuit breaker resets, a CIRCUIT_BREAKER event is emitted (with payload indicating 'resumed'), and
trading can resume. Existing orders that were on the book when the circuit breaker triggered remain on
the book — they are not cancelled — but cannot be matched until trading resumes.
15.6 Order Lifecycle
Orders move through a defined lifecycle: open (order is on the book, no matches yet), partial (order
has been partially filled, remaining quantity is still on the book), filled (order has been completely

---END---
PAGE_199
AVERON
Rishabh Gupta
AVERON PROTOCOL SERIES  ·  VOL. I
198
filled, no remaining quantity), cancelled (order was cancelled by the user before being filled). The
lifecycle is tracked in the status field of the coin_orders table.
Users can cancel their own open or partial orders via the DELETE /api/market/order/:id endpoint.
Cancellation removes the order from the book and sets its status to 'cancelled'. The remaining quantity
is returned to the user's wallet (it was never actually deducted — orders on the book represent
commitments, not deductions). An ORDER_CANCELLED event is emitted on the event bus.
15.7 The Order Book API
The /api/market/orderbook endpoint returns the current state of the order book. The response contains:
bids (array of {price, quantity, order_count} for each price level on the bid side, sorted descending by
price), asks (array of {price, quantity, order_count} for each price level on the ask side, sorted ascending
by price), spread (difference between best bid and best ask), midpoint (average of best bid and best
ask). The endpoint is public — no authentication required — and is typically polled by clients every few
seconds to refresh the order book display.
Real-time order book updates are pushed via the WebSocket 'orders' channel. When a new order is
placed (ORDER_PLACED event) or an existing order is cancelled or filled (ORDER_CANCELLED
event or TRADE_EXECUTED event), the WebSocket server pushes the update to subscribers on the
'orders' channel. This allows clients to maintain a real-time view of the order book without polling.
15.8 Summary
This chapter has documented Averon's trading engine: the order book matching rules (price-time
priority, maker-price-wins, no self-trade, partial fills), order types (limit and market), the matching
algorithm, trade execution (five atomic actions), the circuit breaker (10% / 1-hour halt, configurable),
order lifecycle (open, partial, filled, cancelled), and the order book API. Chapter 16 covers the payment
gateways that move fiat into and out of the platform.
KEY TAKEAWAYS
• 4 matching rules: price-time priority, maker price wins, no self-trade, partial fills
supported.
• 2 order types: limit (price-specified, rests on book if unfilled) and market
(quantity-specified, walks the book).
• Trade execution = 5 atomic actions: trade record, order updates, blockchain tx, fee
collection, price update.

---END---
PAGE_200
AVERON
Rishabh Gupta
AVERON PROTOCOL SERIES  ·  VOL. I
199
• Circuit breaker: 10% price move in 1 hour halts trading; configurable via system_config.
• Order lifecycle: open → partial → filled / cancelled.
• Real-time order book updates via WebSocket 'orders' channel (ORDER_PLACED,
ORDER_CANCELLED events).
Discussion Questions
The circuit breaker halts at 10% / 1 hour. What would be the impact of lowering the threshold to 5%?
Raising it to 20%?
Averon prevents self-trading (buy.user_id === sell.user_id). What other manipulative patterns should the
trading engine detect?
The matching algorithm uses maker-price-wins semantics. What alternative semantics exist, and what are
their trade-offs?

---END---

AVERON

CHAPTER 30
Payments, Fiat Gateways &
Withdrawals
Four payment gateways (Razorpay, Stripe, UPI, Wire), double-mint prevention, and the
eight-step withdrawal flow.
Averon supports four payment gateways for converting fiat currency to AVR coin:
Razorpay, Stripe, UPI, and wire transfer. Each gateway has different currencies,
ranges, and verification mechanisms. This chapter documents the gateways, the
minting flow with its critical double-mint prevention mechanism, and the eight-step
withdrawal flow.
16.1 The Four Payment Gateways
Averon supports four payment gateways, each with different capabilities. The choice of gateway
depends on the user's location, currency, and transaction size.

Table 16.1 — Supported Payment Gateways
Razorpay is the primary gateway for Indian users, supporting INR with a wide range (1 to 25 crore
rupees). Razorpay uses HMAC-SHA256 signature verification — every webhook callback is signed
with a secret shared between Averon and Razorpay, and the signature is verified before any action is
taken. Stripe is the primary gateway for international users, supporting INR, USD, EUR, and GBP with
a range of 1 to 10 crore rupees equivalent. Stripe uses PaymentIntent status verification — the Averon
backend polls the Stripe API to confirm that the PaymentIntent has succeeded before minting coins.

---END---
PAGE_202
AVERON
Rishabh Gupta
AVERON PROTOCOL SERIES  ·  VOL. I
201
UPI is a lightweight gateway for small INR transactions (1 to 2 lakh rupees), with admin confirmation
required before minting. Wire transfer is the gateway for large international transactions (up to 1000
crore rupees equivalent), also with admin confirmation.
16.2 The Minting Flow
The minting flow converts fiat currency to AVR coin. It has eight steps, each of which must succeed for
the minting to complete. The flow is designed to be idempotent — if any step fails and is retried, the
result is the same as if it had succeeded on the first attempt. This is essential because payment gateways
can send duplicate webhooks, and the system must not mint coins twice for the same fiat payment.
Figure 16.1 — Payment Gateway Integration Flow. Fiat-to-AC minting pipeline with double-mint prevention via atomic
status locking.
Step 1: Create Order
The user calls POST /api/payment/create-order with the desired fiat amount and gateway. The
system creates a payment_orders entry with status 'created', recording the user_id, gateway,
fiat_amount, coin_amount (calculated at the current exchange rate), exchange_rate, and kyc_tier (the
user's tier at order creation time, used for limit enforcement).
Step 2: Gateway Initialization
The system calls the gateway's SDK to initialize the payment. For Razorpay, this creates a Razorpay
order with the specified amount and currency. For Stripe, this creates a PaymentIntent with the

---END---
PAGE_203
AVERON
Rishabh Gupta
AVERON PROTOCOL SERIES  ·  VOL. I
202
specified amount and currency. The gateway returns a gateway_order_id, which is stored in the
payment_orders entry.
Step 3: User Pays
The user is redirected to the gateway's hosted payment page, where they enter their payment details
and complete the payment. The gateway processes the payment and, on success, sends a webhook
callback to the Averon backend (POST /api/webhook/:gateway).
Step 4: Webhook
The Averon backend receives the webhook callback. For Razorpay, the webhook is verified using
HMAC-SHA256 signature verification — the signature in the X-Razorpay-Signature header must
match HMAC-SHA256(webhook_body, webhook_secret). For Stripe, the webhook is verified using
Stripe's signature verification library. If verification fails, the webhook is rejected with a 400 error.
Step 5: Atomic Lock
Before minting coins, the system acquires an atomic lock on the payment order: UPDATE
payment_orders SET status = 'verifying' WHERE id = ? AND status IN ('created', 'pending'). If the
affected row count is zero, the order was already being processed by a concurrent request (perhaps a
duplicate webhook), and the system throws an error without minting any coins. This is the double-mint
prevention mechanism — it is impossible to mint coins twice for the same payment order, even if the
gateway sends duplicate webhooks.
Step 6: Verify
The system verifies the payment via the gateway's API. For Razorpay, this calls
razypay.payments.fetch(payment_id) and checks that the status is 'captured'. For Stripe, this calls
stripe.paymentIntents.retrieve(payment_intent_id) and checks that the status is 'succeeded'. Verification
via the API (rather than trusting the webhook alone) provides defense in depth — even if an attacker
could forge a webhook, they could not forge the gateway's API response.
Step 7: Mint AC
The system signs a MINT transaction transferring the coin_amount from the __SYSTEM__ wallet
to the user's wallet. The transaction is added to the mempool and mined into the next block. The user's
wallet balance increases by the coin_amount.
Step 8: Update

---END---
PAGE_204
AVERON
Rishabh Gupta
AVERON PROTOCOL SERIES  ·  VOL. I
203
The payment_orders entry is updated to status 'completed' with the gateway_tx_id and completed_at
timestamp. A COINS_MINTED event is emitted on the event bus, which triggers the priceService to
recalculate the AC price (Mechanism 1: supply-driven recalculation). An audit log entry is created with
the minting details.
16.3 Double-Mint Prevention
Double-mint prevention is the most critical safety mechanism in the payment flow. Without it, a
malicious user (or a buggy gateway) could trigger multiple minting operations for the same fiat
payment, resulting in the user receiving double (or triple, or more) the AC they paid for.
The mechanism is implemented via a single SQL statement: UPDATE payment_orders SET status =
'verifying' WHERE id = ? AND status IN ('created', 'pending'). The WHERE clause is critical — it
ensures that only orders in the 'created' or 'pending' status can be transitioned to 'verifying'. If a second
verification request arrives for the same order (because the order has already been transitioned to
'verifying' by the first request), the WHERE clause fails, the affected row count is zero, and the system
throws an error without minting any coins.
DOUBLE-MINT PREVENTION — THE CRITICAL SQL
UPDATE payment_orders SET status = 'verifying' WHERE id = ? AND status IN ('created',
'pending')
If a second verification request arrives, changes = 0 → throws error → no coins minted twice. This
is enforced at the database level, which is the only place it can be truly atomic across concurrent
requests.
16.4 The Withdrawal Flow
The withdrawal flow converts AVR coin back to fiat currency. It has eight steps and requires admin
processing for the bank transfer portion. The flow is designed to be thoroughly audited — every step is
recorded in the audit log, and the entire flow can be reconstructed from the audit trail.

---END---
PAGE_205
AVERON
Rishabh Gupta
AVERON PROTOCOL SERIES  ·  VOL. I
204
Figure 16.2 — Withdrawal & Settlement Flow. 8-step process for converting AC back to fiat with full KYC/AML
compliance, including periodic reconciliation.
Step 1: Request
The user calls POST /api/withdraw/request with the desired AC amount. The system checks that the
user's KYC tier is at least Tier 1 (Basic KYC required for withdrawals). The withdrawal_requests entry
is created with status 'pending', recording the user_id, coin_amount, fiat_amount (calculated at the
current exchange rate), bank_account details, and kyc_tier.
Step 2: Balance Check
The system verifies that the user's wallet has sufficient AC balance to cover the withdrawal amount
plus the withdrawal fee (0.5 AC). If the balance is insufficient, the request is rejected.
Step 3: Fee Deduction
The withdrawal fee of 0.5 AC is calculated. This fee is sent to the __PLATFORM_FEE__ wallet
via a FEE transaction when the withdrawal is processed (Step 6).
Step 4: Net Calculation
The net amount is calculated: net = coin_amount - 0.5 AC. The net amount is converted to fiat at the
current exchange rate: fiat_amount = net × current_price. The fiat_amount is stored in the
withdrawal_requests entry.
Step 5: Pending Status

---END---
PAGE_206
AVERON
Rishabh Gupta
AVERON PROTOCOL SERIES  ·  VOL. I
205
The withdrawal request enters 'pending' status and becomes visible in the admin withdrawal queue.
The user can monitor the status via GET /api/withdraw/history.
Step 6: Admin Process
An admin reviews the withdrawal request and calls POST /api/withdraw/process/:id. The system
signs two blockchain transactions: a TRANSFER transaction transferring the AC amount from the
user's wallet to the __SYSTEM__ wallet (effectively burning the coins), and a FEE transaction
transferring 0.5 AC from the user's wallet to the __PLATFORM_FEE__ wallet. Both transactions are
mined into the next block. The database balances are updated: the user's wallet balance decreases, the
__SYSTEM__ wallet balance increases (but is not counted in circulating supply), the
__PLATFORM_FEE__ wallet balance increases.
Step 7: Bank Transfer
The admin initiates a bank transfer for the fiat_amount to the user's bank account. The
gateway_tx_id (bank reference number) is recorded in the withdrawal_requests entry.
Step 8: Complete
After the bank transfer is confirmed, the admin calls POST /api/withdraw/complete/:id. The
withdrawal_requests entry is updated to status 'completed' with the completed_at timestamp. The
economy table is updated: circulating_supply is decremented by the coin_amount (the coins are
effectively burned), total_supply is decremented. An audit log entry is created.
16.5 Reconciliation
Reconciliation is a periodic process that checks for discrepancies between internal records and gateway
records. For each payment order with status 'completed', the system computes the internal amount
(coin_amount × exchange_rate_at_time) and fetches the gateway amount from the gateway's API. If
the difference exceeds a threshold (configurable via system_config), a reconciliation_log entry is
created and an admin is alerted.
Reconciliation runs daily (configurable) and serves two purposes. First, it detects operational errors
— for example, if the exchange rate at minting time was incorrect, the internal amount and gateway
amount will differ. Second, it detects fraud — if an attacker managed to mint coins without a
corresponding fiat payment, the gateway amount will be zero while the internal amount is non-zero, and
the discrepancy will be caught by reconciliation.

---END---
PAGE_207
AVERON
Rishabh Gupta
AVERON PROTOCOL SERIES  ·  VOL. I
206
16.6 Failure Paths
Several failure paths are handled gracefully by the payment and withdrawal flows. Failed bank
transfer: the admin marks the withdrawal as 'failed' via POST /api/withdraw/fail/:id, and the AC is
returned to the user's wallet via a REFUND transaction. Failed gateway verification: the payment
order stays in 'pending' status and is auto-refunded after a 24-hour timeout. AML flag triggered: if the
compliance service's AML screening flags the transaction, the request is blocked, the user is notified,
and an admin is alerted for review.
16.7 Summary
This chapter has documented Averon's payment infrastructure: the four payment gateways (Razorpay,
Stripe, UPI, Wire), the eight-step minting flow with double-mint prevention via atomic status locking,
the eight-step withdrawal flow with admin processing, reconciliation for detecting discrepancies, and
the failure paths for bank transfer failures, gateway verification failures, and AML flag triggers. This
concludes Part IV of the book. Part V covers compliance, security, and identity — the four-tier KYC
system, the eight-flag AML screening engine, and the defense-in-depth security model.
KEY TAKEAWAYS
• 4 payment gateways: Razorpay (INR, HMAC-SHA256), Stripe (multi-currency,
PaymentIntent), UPI (INR, admin confirm), Wire (large, admin confirm).
• 8-step minting flow: create order → gateway init → user pays → webhook → atomic lock →
verify → mint AC → update.
• Double-mint prevention: UPDATE payment_orders SET status='verifying' WHERE id=?
AND status IN ('created','pending').
• 8-step withdrawal flow: request → balance check → fee deduction → net calculation →
pending → admin process → bank transfer → complete.
• Periodic reconciliation: compares internal amounts to gateway amounts, logs discrepancies.
• Failure paths: failed bank transfer (REFUND tx), failed verification (auto-refund 24h),
AML flag (blocked, admin alert).
Discussion Questions
Double-mint prevention uses an atomic UPDATE. What additional safeguards would you add for
high-value transactions?

---END---
PAGE_208
AVERON
Rishabh Gupta
AVERON PROTOCOL SERIES  ·  VOL. I
207
The withdrawal flow requires admin processing. How would you design a fully automated withdrawal
flow, and what risks would it introduce?
Reconciliation runs daily. What patterns would warrant more frequent reconciliation, and how would you
implement them?

CHAPTER 31
KYC Tiers & AML Screening
The four-tier KYC system that progressively unlocks transaction limits, and the eight-flag AML
screening engine that runs on every purchase.
Compliance is not a feature bolted on after the fact — it is woven into every layer
of the Averon platform. This chapter documents the four-tier KYC system that
progressively unlocks transaction limits, and the eight-flag AML screening engine
that evaluates every transaction for suspicious patterns.
17.1 The Four-Tier KYC System
Averon uses a four-tier KYC system that progressively unlocks transaction limits as users provide more
verification. The system is designed to balance accessibility (users can start transacting at Tier 0 with
minimal friction) with security (large transactions require extensive verification). Each tier has specific
requirements, transaction limits, and accepted documents.
Figure 17.1 — Four-Tier KYC System. Progressive verification unlocks progressively higher transaction limits. Tier 0
(Unverified, ₹20 daily) to Tier 3 (Institutional, ₹10 Cr daily).
Tier 0: Unverified

---END---
PAGE_212
AVERON
Rishabh Gupta
AVERON PROTOCOL SERIES  ·  VOL. I
211
Tier 0 is the default tier assigned to new users at registration. No documents are required.
Transaction limits are minimal: 20 rupees daily, 20 rupees monthly, 20 rupees annually. In development
mode, Tier 0 is auto-granted with elevated limits for testing; in production, Tier 0 limits are
intentionally restrictive to encourage users to complete KYC. Tier 0 users can browse assets and view
the marketplace but cannot meaningfully transact.
Tier 1: Basic KYC
Tier 1 requires one document submission (Aadhaar, PAN, passport, driving license, or voter ID),
three completed trades, and seven days of account age. Transaction limits: 2 lakh rupees daily, 10 lakh
rupees monthly, 250 lakh rupees annually. Tier 1 is sufficient for retail investors who want to
participate in asset tokenization and trading at moderate scale.
Tier 2: Full KYC
Tier 2 requires 10 completed trades, 1 lakh rupees in trading volume, and 30 days of account age.
Transaction limits: 10 lakh rupees daily, 1 crore rupees monthly, 5 crore rupees annually. Tier 2 is
suitable for serious retail investors and small institutional participants.
Tier 3: Institutional
Tier 3 requires 50 completed trades, 1 crore rupees in trading volume, and 90 days of account age.
Accepted documents include GST certificate and incorporation certificate (in addition to the
documents accepted at lower tiers). Transaction limits: 10 crore rupees daily, 100 crore rupees monthly,
1000 crore rupees annually. Tier 3 is designed for institutional participants — banks, asset managers,
corporate treasuries.
17.2 KYC Document Submission and Review
Users submit KYC documents via POST /api/kyc/submit, which creates a kyc_records entry with status
'pending'. The submission includes doc_type (one of: Aadhaar, PAN, passport, driving license, voter
ID, GST certificate, incorporation certificate), doc_number, and an image of the document. The system
stores the document image in uploads/kyc/ with a crypto-randomized filename.
KYC submissions are reviewed by admins via the admin panel. The admin can approve or reject
each submission via POST /api/kyc/verify/:recordId, providing a reason for rejection. If approved, the
user's kyc_status is updated to 'verified' and their kyc_tier is incremented. A kyc_tier_history entry is
created with old_tier, new_tier, and reason. If rejected, the user is notified and can resubmit with
corrected documents.

---END---
PAGE_213
AVERON
Rishabh Gupta
AVERON PROTOCOL SERIES  ·  VOL. I
212
17.3 Daily Limit Tracking
Daily transaction totals are tracked in the daily_limits table to enforce per-day caps. Each row has:
user_id, date, total_bought (sum of all fiat-to-AC purchases on that date), tx_count (number of
transactions on that date). Before each transaction, the system checks that the proposed transaction
would not exceed the user's tier limit for the current day, month, or year. If the transaction would
exceed any limit, it is rejected with an error indicating which limit would be exceeded.
Monthly and annual limits are computed by summing daily_limits entries for the relevant period.
This is more efficient than querying every individual transaction, and it provides a clear audit trail of
limit enforcement. The daily_limits table is updated atomically with each transaction, ensuring that
concurrent transactions cannot both pass the limit check and then collectively exceed the limit.
17.4 The Eight AML Flag Types
The AML (Anti-Money-Laundering) screening engine evaluates every purchase transaction against
eight flag types. Each flag represents a specific pattern of suspicious activity. If three or more flags are
triggered simultaneously, the transaction is blocked, the user is notified, and an admin is alerted for
review.
Figure 17.2 — AML Transaction Screening Engine. Eight AML flag types evaluated on every purchase; 3+ simultaneous
flags trigger a block.

---END---
PAGE_214
AVERON
Rishabh Gupta
AVERON PROTOCOL SERIES  ·  VOL. I
213
Flag 1: HIGH_VALUE
Triggered when the transaction amount is greater than or equal to 10 lakh rupees. Large transactions
are not inherently suspicious, but they warrant additional scrutiny because they represent a significant
movement of funds. A single HIGH_VALUE flag does not block the transaction, but it contributes to
the flag count.
Flag 2: ROUND_AMOUNT
Triggered when the transaction amount is a round number greater than or equal to 5 lakh rupees
(e.g., 10,00,000, 20,00,000). Round amounts are suspicious because they suggest the transaction was
structured for accounting convenience rather than reflecting an organic economic activity. Criminals
often use round amounts to simplify money laundering accounting.
Flag 3: RAPID_SEQUENTIAL
Triggered when the user has made three or more transactions within 60 seconds. Rapid sequential
transactions suggest automated activity, which is suspicious because normal users do not typically
submit multiple transactions in such a short window. Bots that structure transactions to evade thresholds
often trigger this flag.
Flag 4: NEW_ACCOUNT_HIGH_VALUE
Triggered when a user transacts 5 lakh rupees or more within 7 days of account creation. New
accounts that immediately move large funds are suspicious because legitimate users typically start with
smaller transactions to test the platform before committing larger amounts. Money launderers often
create new accounts specifically for a single large transaction.
Flag 5: HIGH_FREQUENCY
Triggered when the user's transaction frequency exceeds their historical baseline by a significant
margin. For example, a user who typically makes 2 transactions per week but suddenly makes 20
transactions in a day would trigger this flag. High frequency can indicate structuring (breaking a large
transaction into many small ones to evade thresholds).
Flag 6: DUPLICATE_PATTERN
Triggered when the user submits repeated identical amounts. For example, three transactions of
99,000 rupees each would trigger this flag (the amounts are identical and just below the 1 lakh reporting
threshold). Duplicate patterns are a strong signal of structuring.
Flag 7: STRUCTURING

---END---
PAGE_215
AVERON
Rishabh Gupta
AVERON PROTOCOL SERIES  ·  VOL. I
214
Triggered when the user submits multiple transactions that are just below a reporting threshold. For
example, three transactions of 49,000 rupees each would trigger this flag (the amounts are just below
the 50,000 rupee threshold). Structuring is a specific money laundering technique designed to evade
reporting requirements.
Flag 8: VELOCITY_CHECK
Triggered when the user's cumulative spend velocity (amount per unit time) exceeds a threshold
derived from their historical pattern. This flag is similar to HIGH_FREQUENCY but focuses on
amount rather than count — a user who typically spends 10,000 rupees per week but suddenly spends
10 lakh rupees in a day would trigger this flag.
17.5 The Blocking Rule
The blocking rule is simple: if three or more AML flags are triggered simultaneously, the transaction is
blocked. This rule is conservative — it is better to block a legitimate transaction (which the user can
appeal) than to allow a fraudulent transaction (which cannot be undone). The threshold of three flags is
configurable via system_config.
AML BLOCKING RULE
flag_count = count of simultaneously-triggered AML flags
if flag_count ≥ 3 → BLOCK transaction, alert admin, freeze account pending review
When a transaction is blocked, the user is notified with a message explaining that the transaction
was flagged for review. The admin is alerted via the admin panel and an email notification. The user's
account is not automatically frozen — they can continue to transact at lower amounts — but the blocked
transaction must be reviewed by an admin before it can be re-attempted. The admin can override the
block (allowing the transaction to proceed) or uphold it (permanently blocking the transaction and
potentially freezing the account).
17.6 Why AML Screening Matters
AML screening is not just a regulatory requirement — it is a fundamental safety mechanism for the
platform. Money laundering is a serious crime that enables other serious crimes (terrorism, drug
trafficking, human trafficking). Platforms that facilitate money laundering — even unintentionally —
can face severe regulatory penalties, including shutdown. More importantly, platforms that become

---END---
PAGE_216
AVERON
Rishabh Gupta
AVERON PROTOCOL SERIES  ·  VOL. I
215
known as money laundering channels lose the trust of legitimate users, which is fatal for a platform that
aims to be the operating system of the digital asset economy.
Averon's AML screening is designed to be both effective and fair. It is effective because it evaluates
every transaction against eight pattern types, catching a wide range of suspicious activity. It is fair
because it uses a multi-flag threshold (three flags required for blocking) rather than blocking on any
single flag, reducing false positives. And it is transparent because every flag trigger is recorded in the
audit log, allowing users and admins to understand why a transaction was blocked.
17.7 Summary
This chapter has documented Averon's compliance layer: the four-tier KYC system (Tier 0 Unverified,
Tier 1 Basic, Tier 2 Full, Tier 3 Institutional) with progressive transaction limits and requirements, the
KYC document submission and review process, daily limit tracking, the eight AML flag types
(HIGH_VALUE, ROUND_AMOUNT, RAPID_SEQUENTIAL, NEW_ACCOUNT_HIGH_VALUE,
HIGH_FREQUENCY, DUPLICATE_PATTERN, STRUCTURING, VELOCITY_CHECK), and the
blocking rule (3+ simultaneous flags). Chapter 18 covers the broader security architecture that
complements the compliance layer.
KEY TAKEAWAYS
• 4-tier KYC system: Tier 0 Unverified (₹20/day) → Tier 1 Basic (₹2L/day) → Tier 2 Full
(₹10L/day) → Tier 3 Institutional (₹10Cr/day).
• Each tier has progressive requirements: documents, trades, volume, account age.
• Daily/monthly/annual limits tracked in daily_limits table; checked before every
transaction.
• 8 AML flag types: HIGH_VALUE, ROUND_AMOUNT, RAPID_SEQUENTIAL,
NEW_ACCOUNT_HIGH_VALUE, HIGH_FREQUENCY, DUPLICATE_PATTERN,
STRUCTURING, VELOCITY_CHECK.
• Blocking rule: 3+ simultaneous AML flags → block transaction, alert admin.
• Threshold of 3 flags reduces false positives while catching real fraud patterns.

participants? What would its requirements be?

CHAPTER 32
Security Architecture
Defense in depth: five concentric layers from perimeter to cryptographic core, protecting the
platform at every level.
Security in a financial platform is not a single mechanism — it is a layered system
of overlapping defenses. Averon uses a defense-in-depth model with five concentric
layers, each protecting a different aspect of the platform. This chapter documents
each layer and the specific mechanisms it implements.
18.1 The Defense-in-Depth Model
Defense in depth is a military concept that has been adopted by cybersecurity: rather than relying on a
single strong defense, multiple weaker defenses are layered so that an attacker must breach all of them
to succeed. If any single layer fails, the other layers still provide protection. Averon's defense-in-depth
model has five concentric layers, from the perimeter (Layer 5) to the cryptographic core (Layer 1).
Figure 18.1 — Defense-in-Depth Security Model. Five concentric layers protect the platform from perimeter to data at rest.
Each layer adds independent protection; breaching any one layer does not compromise the platform.

---END---
PAGE_219
AVERON
Rishabh Gupta
AVERON PROTOCOL SERIES  ·  VOL. I
218
18.2 Layer 5: Perimeter
The perimeter layer is the outermost defense, protecting the platform from network-level attacks. It
consists of three mechanisms: HTTP security headers (via Helmet.js), CORS (Cross-Origin Resource
Sharing) configuration, and rate limiting.
Helmet.js sets a dozen HTTP headers that protect against common web attacks:
Content-Security-Policy (prevents XSS by restricting script sources), Strict-Transport-Security (forces
HTTPS), X-Frame-Options (prevents clickjacking), X-Content-Type-Options (prevents MIME type
sniffing), and others. These headers are set automatically by Helmet.js with secure defaults, and can be
customized via the security.js configuration file.
CORS configuration restricts which origins can make requests to the Averon API. By default, only
the Averon web frontend origin is allowed; additional origins can be added via configuration. CORS
prevents malicious websites from making authenticated requests to the Averon API on behalf of users
who have logged in to Averon in another browser tab.
Rate limiting is implemented as a four-tier token bucket, with each tier applied to a different
category of endpoint: generalLimiter (100 requests per minute, all routes), authLimiter (10 requests per
minute, login and register), financialLimiter (5 requests per second, buy/sell/trade/withdraw),
uploadLimiter (10 requests per minute, document uploads). Rate limiting prevents abuse — brute-force
password attacks, transaction spam, denial-of-service attacks — without penalizing normal usage
patterns.
18.3 Layer 4: Authentication
The authentication layer verifies the identity of users. It consists of three mechanisms: JWT
authentication, PBKDF2 password hashing, and account lockout.
JWT authentication uses HMAC-SHA256 signed tokens with zero external dependencies (the
JWT implementation is custom, using Node's crypto module). Access tokens expire in 15 minutes and
are sent in the Authorization header as Bearer tokens. Refresh tokens expire in 7 days and are stored in
the sessions table to support revocation. The custom implementation avoids supply chain risk from
third-party JWT libraries.
PBKDF2 password hashing uses SHA-512 with 100,000 iterations and a 32-byte random salt per
user. PBKDF2 is a key derivation function designed to be slow, making brute-force attacks on stolen
password hashes computationally expensive. The 100,000 iteration count is configurable and should be

---END---
PAGE_220
AVERON
Rishabh Gupta
AVERON PROTOCOL SERIES  ·  VOL. I
219
increased over time as hardware speeds improve. The salt prevents rainbow table attacks — even if two
users have the same password, their hashes are different because their salts are different.
Account lockout locks an account for 15 minutes after 5 failed login attempts. This prevents
brute-force password attacks — an attacker cannot try more than 5 passwords per 15-minute window
per account. The lockout is automatic and is lifted after 15 minutes without admin intervention.
Account lockout is tracked in the users table via a failed_login_count field and a locked_until
timestamp.
18.4 Layer 3: Authorization and Validation
The authorization layer ensures that authenticated users can only perform actions they are authorized to
perform. It consists of three mechanisms: role-based access control, input validation, and input
sanitization.
Role-based access control is implemented via the requireRole(role) and requireAdmin
middleware. Each route specifies the required role (or roles) and the middleware checks that
req.user.role matches. The 'admin' role has access to all admin endpoints (freeze/unfreeze accounts,
system config, KYC review, withdrawal processing); the 'user' role has access to standard user
endpoints (assets, trading, payments, portfolio).
Input validation ensures that request bodies match expected schemas. The validator middleware
checks: register (email format, password strength minimum 8 characters, name length), login (email and
password presence), createAsset (title, category, raiseAmount bounds), buyTokens (positive count),
placeOrder (side, amount, price validation). Invalid input is rejected with a 400 error and a descriptive
message.
Input sanitization strips potentially dangerous characters from string body fields: script tags, dollar
signs, curly braces. This prevents XSS attacks (script tags), shell injection (dollar signs), and template
injection (curly braces). Sanitization is applied uniformly to all string fields, regardless of whether the
field is expected to contain such characters — defense in depth.
18.5 Layer 2: Business Logic Safeguards
The business logic layer implements safety mechanisms that prevent logical errors from causing
financial harm. It consists of five mechanisms: double-mint prevention, race condition protection,
circuit breaker, KYC tier enforcement, and AML flag screening.

---END---
PAGE_221
AVERON
Rishabh Gupta
AVERON PROTOCOL SERIES  ·  VOL. I
220
Double-mint prevention uses atomic UPDATE on the payment_orders table to ensure that each
fiat payment mints AC exactly once. Race condition protection uses UPDATE WHERE owner_id IS
NULL to ensure that each asset token is claimed by exactly one investor. Circuit breaker halts trading
when prices move more than 10% in 1 hour. KYC tier enforcement checks that the user's tier supports
the proposed transaction amount. AML flag screening evaluates every purchase against eight
suspicious pattern types. These mechanisms are documented in detail in their respective chapters.
18.6 Layer 1: Cryptographic Core
The cryptographic core is the innermost layer, providing the cryptographic primitives on which the
platform's security rests. It consists of five mechanisms: ECDSA secp256k1 signing, SHA-256
proof-of-work, hash-chained audit log, Merkle tree verification, and webhook HMAC verification.
ECDSA secp256k1 signing is the same elliptic curve used by Bitcoin and Ethereum, validated by
over a decade of production use and billions of dollars of value secured. Every blockchain transaction is
signed by its sender, and the signature is verified before the transaction is added to the mempool.
SHA-256 proof-of-work makes the blockchain expensive to attack — rewriting history requires
recomputing all the PoW, which is prohibitively expensive. Hash-chained audit log makes the audit
trail tamper-evident — any modification to an audit entry breaks the chain and is detectable. Merkle
tree verification enables efficient proof-of-inclusion for transactions without downloading the full
block. Webhook HMAC verification ensures that webhooks from payment gateways are authentic
and have not been tampered with.
18.7 The Security Feature Inventory
The defense-in-depth model is implemented via a comprehensive set of security features, each
addressing a specific threat. The table below summarizes the features by category.

18.8 Threat Model and Mitigations
The security architecture is designed to mitigate a specific threat model. The threats considered include:
external attackers trying to steal funds or compromise user accounts, malicious users trying to game the
platform's economics (double-mint, double-claim, wash trading), insider threats from administrators
with database access, and supply chain attacks from third-party dependencies.
External attackers are mitigated by the perimeter layer (rate limiting, security headers) and
authentication layer (PBKDF2, JWT, account lockout). An attacker who compromises a user's
password still cannot access the user's wallet without the wallet's private key, which is stored in the
database and never exposed via the API. Malicious users are mitigated by the business logic
safeguards (double-mint prevention, race condition protection, circuit breaker, AML screening). A user
who tries to double-mint AC is blocked by the atomic UPDATE; a user who tries to double-claim
tokens is blocked by the WHERE owner_id IS NULL clause; a user who tries to manipulate prices is
blocked by the circuit breaker.
Insider threats are mitigated by the cryptographic core (hash-chained audit log, ECDSA signing).
An administrator with database access cannot modify the audit log without breaking the hash chain, and
cannot transfer funds without the user's private key (which is stored in the database but is encrypted at
rest in production deployments). Supply chain attacks are mitigated by the minimal dependency count
(7 production dependencies) and the custom JWT implementation (eliminating the most common
supply chain attack vector for Node.js applications).

---END---
PAGE_223
AVERON
Rishabh Gupta
AVERON PROTOCOL SERIES  ·  VOL. I
222
18.9 Summary
This chapter has documented Averon's security architecture: the defense-in-depth model with five
concentric layers (perimeter, authentication, authorization and validation, business logic safeguards,
cryptographic core), the specific mechanisms in each layer, the security feature inventory, and the
threat model and mitigations. Chapter 19 covers the tamper-proof audit log and governance system that
complement the security architecture.
KEY TAKEAWAYS
• Defense-in-depth: 5 concentric layers from perimeter to cryptographic core.
• Layer 5 (Perimeter): Helmet.js headers, CORS, 4-tier rate limiting.
• Layer 4 (Authentication): PBKDF2-SHA512 (100K iter), JWT (HMAC-SHA256,
zero-dep), account lockout (5 fails → 15min).
• Layer 3 (Authorization & Validation): requireRole, schema validation, input sanitization
(strip script tags, $, {, }).
• Layer 2 (Business Logic): double-mint prevention, race condition protection, circuit
breaker, KYC tier enforcement, AML screening.
• Layer 1 (Cryptographic Core): ECDSA secp256k1, SHA-256 PoW, hash-chained audit,
Merkle verification, webhook HMAC.

would an HSM-based alternative look like?
How would you extend the security model to protect against social engineering attacks (e.g., an attacker
convincing an admin to process a fraudulent withdrawal)?

CHAPTER 33
Tamper-Proof Audit & Governance
The hash-chained audit log that records every state-changing operation, and the admin
operations that govern the platform.
Trust in a financial platform ultimately rests on auditability — the ability to
reconstruct what happened, when, and by whom. Averon's audit log is
hash-chained, making it tamper-evident: any modification to an entry breaks the
chain and is immediately detectable. This chapter documents the audit log, the API
route map, and the admin operations that govern the platform.
19.1 The Hash-Chained Audit Log
Every state-changing request (POST, PUT, PATCH, DELETE) on the Averon platform is recorded in
the audit_log table. The recording is performed by the auditMiddleware, which sits in the middleware
pipeline after authentication and before the route handler. Each audit log entry contains: action (string
describing the action), details (JSON with request body, response status, and other relevant data),
user_id (the authenticated user), resource_type (the type of resource affected), resource_id (the ID of
the specific resource), timestamp (ISO 8601 with millisecond precision), prev_hash (the entry_hash of
the previous audit log entry), entry_hash (SHA-256 hash of this entry).

---END---
PAGE_225
AVERON
Rishabh Gupta
AVERON PROTOCOL SERIES  ·  VOL. I
224
Figure 19.1 — Tamper-Proof Audit Log (Hash Chain). Every state-changing request appends a hash-chained entry.
Tampering breaks the chain detectably. The verifyAuditChain() function iterates all entries and detects any corruption.
19.2 The Hash Chain Formula
The entry_hash is computed as: SHA-256(prev_hash + user_id + action + resource_type + resource_id
+ timestamp). This formula binds each entry to the previous entry (via prev_hash) and to all of its own
content. If any field is modified — the action, the user_id, the timestamp — the entry_hash no longer
matches the stored value, and the tampering is detectable.
HASH CHAIN FORMULA
entry_hash = SHA-256( prev_hash : user_id : action : resource_type : resource_id : timestamp )
verifyAuditChain() iterates all entries; if any entry's computed hash ≠ stored hash → corruption
detected
The prev_hash field creates the chain: each entry references the previous entry's hash, so modifying
any entry breaks all subsequent entries (their prev_hash no longer matches the modified entry's hash).
This is the same mechanism used by blockchains — each block references the previous block's hash,
making the chain tamper-evident. The audit log is effectively a private blockchain, recording every
state-changing operation on the platform.
19.3 The verifyAuditChain Function
The verifyAuditChain() function iterates all entries in the audit_log table and verifies the hash chain.
For each entry, it recomputes the entry_hash from the entry's fields (prev_hash, user_id, action,
resource_type, resource_id, timestamp) and compares it to the stored entry_hash. If any entry's
computed hash does not match the stored hash, the function returns false and reports the corrupted
entry's ID.
verifyAuditChain() is called automatically as part of the /api/admin/stats endpoint, which is
typically polled by admins every few minutes. If the function returns false, the admin stats response
includes a 'audit_chain_corrupted' flag, alerting the admin to investigate. The function can also be
called manually via a CLI script for periodic integrity checks.
19.4 The API Route Map

---END---
PAGE_226
AVERON
Rishabh Gupta
AVERON PROTOCOL SERIES  ·  VOL. I
225

19.5 Admin Operations
Admins have access to a set of operations that are not available to regular users. These operations are
grouped into five categories: dashboard stats, account management, system configuration, KYC review,
and withdrawal processing.
Dashboard Stats
GET /api/admin/stats returns a comprehensive dashboard with economy metrics (price, circulating
supply, total raised, market cap, TVL, fees collected), blockchain info (chain height, latest block hash,
transaction count), audit integrity (result of verifyAuditChain()), and pending counts (pending assets,
pending KYC submissions, pending withdrawals). The dashboard is the admin's primary monitoring
tool.
Account Management

---END---
PAGE_227
AVERON
Rishabh Gupta
AVERON PROTOCOL SERIES  ·  VOL. I
226
POST /api/admin/freeze/:userId freezes a user account, preventing all outbound transactions. POST
/api/admin/unfreeze/:userId restores the user's access. Frozen accounts can still receive incoming
transactions but cannot send. Account freezing is used in response to suspected fraud, AML flag
triggers, or user requests (e.g., a user who suspects their account has been compromised can request
freezing).
System Configuration
POST /api/admin/config updates system configuration values stored in the system_config table.
Configurable values include: trading_fee_percent, capital_raise_fee_percent, withdrawal_fee_ac,
circuit_breaker_threshold_percent, 
circuit_breaker_window_minutes, 
min_raise_amount_inr,
max_raise_amount_inr, and others. Configuration changes take effect immediately and are recorded in
the audit log.
KYC Review
GET /api/kyc/pending returns the list of pending KYC submissions. POST /api/kyc/verify/:recordId
approves or rejects a submission with a reason. Approved submissions increment the user's kyc_tier;
rejected submissions notify the user to resubmit with corrected documents.
Withdrawal Processing
GET /api/withdraw/pending returns the list of pending withdrawal requests. POST
/api/withdraw/process/:id processes a withdrawal (signs and mines the TRANSFER and FEE
transactions, updates database balances). POST /api/withdraw/complete/:id marks a withdrawal as
completed (after the bank transfer is confirmed). POST /api/withdraw/fail/:id rejects a withdrawal
(returns AC to the user via a REFUND transaction). Withdrawal processing is the most operationally
intensive admin task.
19.6 Governance
AVR holders participate in protocol governance by voting on parameter changes, fee adjustments, and
roadmap priorities. Voting weight is proportional to AVR holdings. Governance proposals are
submitted via the developer platform API and require a quorum of votes to pass. The governance
system is currently advisory — the core team retains final decision-making authority — but Phase 2 of
the roadmap transitions to a community DAO model where governance decisions are binding.
Governance proposals fall into three categories. Parameter changes propose modifications to
system_config values (e.g., increasing the trading fee from 0.1% to 0.15%, or adjusting the circuit
breaker threshold). Roadmap priorities propose reordering or adding items to the development

---END---
PAGE_228
AVERON
Rishabh Gupta
AVERON PROTOCOL SERIES  ·  VOL. I
227
roadmap (e.g., prioritizing cross-chain bridges over institutional custody). Ecosystem grants propose
allocating AVR from the platform treasury to support third-party development (e.g., funding a
developer to build a mobile wallet).
Each proposal has a voting period (typically 7 days), during which AVR holders can vote yes or no.
The voting weight is the voter's AVR balance at the start of the voting period (snapshot voting),
preventing last-minute vote buying. A proposal passes if it receives more yes votes than no votes AND
the yes votes constitute a quorum (typically 10% of circulating supply). Passed proposals are
implemented by the core team (in the current advisory model) or executed automatically by the protocol
(in the planned DAO model).
19.7 Summary
This chapter has documented Averon's audit and governance systems: the hash-chained audit log with
the SHA-256 chain formula and verifyAuditChain() function, the 40+ API endpoints organized into ten
functional groups, the five categories of admin operations (dashboard stats, account management,
system configuration, KYC review, withdrawal processing), and the governance system (parameter
changes, roadmap priorities, ecosystem grants, with snapshot voting and quorum requirements). This
concludes Part V of the book. Part VI covers use cases and the developer platform.
KEY TAKEAWAYS
• Audit log: every state-changing request (POST/PUT/PATCH/DELETE) recorded with
hash-chained entries.
• Hash chain formula: entry_hash = SHA-256(prev_hash : user_id : action : resource_type :
resource_id : timestamp).
• verifyAuditChain() iterates all entries; mismatched hash → corruption detected.
• 40+ API endpoints organized into 10 functional groups (config, auth, account, payments,
KYC, withdrawals, assets, market, portfolio/blockchain, admin/economy).
• 5 admin operation categories: dashboard stats, account management, system config, KYC
review, withdrawal processing.
• Governance: 3 proposal types (parameter changes, roadmap priorities, ecosystem grants),
snapshot voting, quorum 10%.
